WOMEN'S CHAT.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

INCREASING LUMINOUS EFFICIENCY.

Raising the temperature of a bare platinum wire from 800 degrees C. to 820 degrees, more I than doubles its brightness, while doubling the temperature of a white-hot body is estimated to increase the - luminous intensity 4,000 times. With this in mind, Adolf Herz, a German, has passed an electric current through an Auer glower of 'metal oxides heated by a gas-flame, I and has thus considerably raised the tempera- ture. The. result is an extraordinary increase in luminous efficiency.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
SUN TEMPERATURE ESTIMATES.

The electric arc in which titanium—a metal- i Lc compound, which burns when heated in the air—has been distilled, has a temperature of about 3,500 degrees C. Making allowance for I uncertainty as to pressure in the solar atmo- 'sphere, M. Moissan conclude that points on the 'sun, where titanium is seen, in the solar ; spectrum, to be volatilised, must have a tem- perature between two estimates ef sun-heat already made-6,590 degrees C., and 2,000 de- grees to 3,000 degrees C. The probability is that the latter is nearer to the true heat of the sun than the former. This is very much lower thap earlier estimates.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
WILD CATS IN IRELAND.

In the proceedings of the Royal Irish Academy, Mr. R. F. Scharff gives some inter- esting evidence regarding the former existence of ,wild cats in Ireland. . Most important ap- pears to be the large collection of cat remains from certain caves near Ennis, in County Clare. He concludes, confidently, that the wild cat did exist in Ireland in comparatively recent times. The Irish wild cat, however; was not the Euro- pean Felis catus," but rather the African "Fens oereata." The latter species has not a bushy tail. It is considered that the majority of the domestic cats of Ireland are descended from this African species.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VISION OF BEETLES.

A notable fact about the vision of insects, and one which it may be supposed must largely influence • their view of the external world, is the number of facets, or lenses, in compound eyes. A German naturalist, K. Leinemann, has been painstaking enough to count the number of facets in the eyes of no fewer than 150 species of beetle. He fines that in the same species and sex the number in- creases with the size of the body. There is usually no permanent difference between the sexes as to the number of facets. Occasionally, however, the difference is marked, as in the case of Lampyris Splendidula, in which the male has 2,500 and the female 300. One species is noted which has the extraordinary number of 24,000 facets in its eye. The number of facets is greater in the rapidly moviitg active forms than in the more sluggish species. ,

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THE DUCHESS OF LEEDS.

The Duchess of Leeds, who has just been keep- ing her birthday, 4 a sister of the Earl of Dur- ham, having been Lady Katherine Lambton be- fore her marriage to the Duke in 1884. She is one of the Peeressses who have challenged literary criticism, ,and has written both novels and poetry. Some readers may remember her "Capriccios" and her "Lover of the Beautiful." She shsces her husband's love of yachting and his fondness for the Riviera, and stays for some months every year at Borrlighera. With very dark hair, she has delicate features and a pale complexion—in fact, a typical Lambton in ap- pearance.

NEAT SCHOOL FROCK.

Summer holidays, alas! are rapidly drawing to a close, and it is high time to be making pre- paration for the coming autumn term. Promi- nent among the schoolgirl's requirements is a neat and serviceable school frock which will stand any amount of wear and tear, and yet present a respectable appearance. The pretty frock pictured in .8 our, sketch is admirably adapted for such a purpose. Fashioned of brown cashmere, in a pretty and becoming shade, it is smart and &linty, and at the same time, pre-eminently iiseful. The bodice is ar- ranged with a plastron front adorned with Linea

of machine-stitching and large brown velvet buttons. On either side of this plastron come wide tucks, also machined at the edges. The sleeves, of tho bishop shape, are quite small and neat, and are finished with plain stitched cuff. A stock of brown silk and a turn-down collar of lawn complete the bodice. The skirt is abso- lutely plain, well gored to secure the necessary width at the hem, and is finished with a smart belt of brown patent leather.

THE FRENCH NURSE.

The British nurse is one of the institutions that the French have most envied, says a writer in the Sketch. Well, they h:ive their own nurses l'Anglaise now. The French "Sairey Camp" was rather a dreadful person. She wore the bibs and tuckers of the sick-room, but her medi- al knowledge you could stow away in that little pocket of hers. She was either too good or not good enough. Either she was a Sister of Mercy trained in a convent, knowing little of body-cure, though versed, doubtless, in the healing of souls, or she was an untrained and inferior person who had no spiritual or any other parts whatever. But both these types are improved away. The French girl,' trained in the lycee and of good bourgeois stock has begun to take an interest in nursing. She has passed her examination and become competent. Presently she will be seen in every hospital in the coun- try. The experiment has begun at Bordeaux. There the French nurse, new style, is under the charge of an English lady, Miss Elston, who has been trained in the London hospitals. It is the happiest arrangement. The nurses are well housed, well clothed, and well fed. Instead of relying on exhortation to recover the patient they are strong in their diploma and their hos- pitail science. AUTUMN SAM BLOISE.

The blouse shown in our illustration is a little shirt-waist that has just been prepared for- autumn wear. It is made of Scotch wincey. It fastens up the back, and has a plain front made with three broad tucks over each shoulder, the plain part in front being strapped across with three graduated straps. The collar is of the mate- rial, and is made over a feather-bone foundation; and the two ribbon rosettes which are placed on

either side of the collar nave long tie ends, which pads underneath the three little straps. Having the tie thus attached to the blouse, the latter can be put on very quickly, without any adjust- ment save the buttons and hooks used for fastening it up the back.

ORNAMENTAL BUTTONS.

The translation of the button from the sphero of use to that of ornament has the confirma- tion of several seasons to render it absolute. This autumn, however, a reaction would seem to be setting in against the embroidered or braided specimen in stuff or silk in favour of ornamental metals, bronze and copper being no less in request than silver, the sine qua non being that the effect shall be generally antique. It is possible, of course, that this craze for old- fashioned ornaments may be one outcome al the recent researches amongst the Old Masters for sartorial inspiration, but at any rate, says the Taller, the, student of advanced Paris modes can do no less than admit that this is ono of the leading features of to-morrow's creations.

CHESHIRE LIBERALS.

A GREAT GATHERING IN ALDERLEY PARK.

Sneech by Mr. A. J. King, M.P.

The delightfully situated country seat of Lord Stanley of Alderley, at Alderley'Park, was the scene of a great gathering of Cheshire Liberals on Saturday. The meeting was ostensibly one of the supporters of Mr. A. J. King, M.P. for the Knutsford division, but there were adherents of the Liberal cause present from other parts of Cheshire. The weather was perfect, and the people wandered at will about the beautiful par]; and gardens. After tea a meeting was held in front of the Hall. Lord Stanley of Alderley presided, and there were present Mr. A. J.. King, M.P., Mr. James Tomkinpon, M.P., Mr. H. Vivian, M.P.' and many leader, of Liberalism in the Knutsford and other Cheshire Parlia- mentary divisions.

THE EDUCATION BILL. Lord Stanley of Alderley, who was loudly cheereil, said in the course of a brief speech that he did not believe the House of Lords would improve anything that came from the House of Commons. They would be at work very soon. All be had to say about the Education Bill was that he would have been very glad to have got something better, and should be sorry to get anything much worse. (Hear, hear.) He did not feel very confident that that Bill would not have a great many holes driven into it and a good many cuts slashed in it, and it would be for the House of Commons to make up their-minds, when the Bill came back to them, how far they might be firm and how far they might give way. He suspected Sir H. Campbell-Bannerman knew that he would be expected by .the House of Commons to let everybody, whether House of Lords, or bishops, or anyone else, know that when the people had sent their representatives to Parliament deter- mined to have something done, they meant to have it. (Hear, bear.) That was the question which had to be settled sooner or later, and he hoped it would be settled sooner. Liberals believed in popular represents- tive government. They believed the voice of the people's representatives should guide and decide the policy of the legislation of the country. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19060915_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INDUSTRIOUS GOVERNMENT.

Mr. J. Tomkinson, M.P., proposed a resolution which recorded the continued confidence of the Liberals of the Knutsford division in the Government. The House of Commons, he said, which was in his opinion the best House that had ever been returned, had not got so bad a record so far. Its members had been industrious,they bad put their hearts into their work, and they had already got through a great deal.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM DRY ROT TO VIGOROUS HEALTH.

Mr. A. J. King, M.P., who was warmly welcomed, seconded the resolution. He said that one result of the splendid majority which was obtained at the last election had been that it had stopped the dry rot which had set ft. (Cheers and laughter.) They could not have found a better man to undertake that work than the present Prime Minister, to whom at this time their hearts turned with deep sympathy. In spite of the peculiarly difficult circumstances under which the Government took office, the House of Commons had shown a very remarkable record of legislation during its first season. The most important measure was of course the Education Bill, and he believed that measure would go through without any very material alteration. Then there were the Plural Voting Bill and the Merchant Shipping Bill, and, among others, the County Magistrates Bill, He hoped, as a result of that measure, something would be done to make the magis- terial body in counties, and especially in Cheshire, a little less one-sided than it was at present. There was certainly room for improvement there without the accusation being made that magistrates were appointed because of party services. With regard to foreign affairs no one could complain of the work of Sir Edward Grey. Since the Government had come into office there had been distinctly better feeling between this country and Germany, without the least interfer- ence with that excellent entente cordiale with our nearest neighbours the French people. With regard to the future there was an immense amount of work to be done, and he believed there was a determination amongst the rank and file of the House of Commons to put aside personal prejudices and support heartily and enthu- siastically such measures as the Government might bring in in furtherance of the great policy the country sent them to carry out. (Cheers).

THB POWER OF POLITICAL HONESTY. Mt. Vivian, M.P. supported the resolution. Speak- ing of the influence of Sir H. Campbell-Bannerman in the House of Commons with all sections of the pro- gressive forces of the House, he said the secret of that influence was summed op in the word honesty—above all, Sir Henry was honest.—(Cheers.) The Conserva- tive party had realised that they had been hampered by having a man at their head who could not declare clearly what their opinions were on the great questions before the country. He had come across Tories who had lamented very much the shuffling of Mr. Balfour. The whole country was relieved when it found at the head of public affairs a man who said straight out what be meant. Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman bad raised the tone of public life, which was a matter of immense importance to this country.—(Cheers). Other speeches were made by Mr. H. P. Greg and Mr. Theodore Crewdson and, in response to calls, Mrs. King said a few words. 'The speakers were thanked for their services, and Lord and Lady Stanley of Alderley were also thanked for permitting the use of their perk for the demonstration.

MAKESHIFT MEALS.

Is there any reason why " makeshift meals " should not be appetising ? A little resourcefultmss will easily render them more attractive. The " Sweets" are generally very poorly represented, yet (as the King's physician reminds us) there is no more delicious and nutritive a sweet than Currant Bread (White or fiovis) or Cake. Currants provide abundant nourishment for brain and body.

Mr. George Joseph Howell, a London business' man who put a commercial traveller out of his office because " Ile could not allow people to abuse him before his own clerks," was tined 60s. and costs for assault on Tuesday, and reminded by the magistrate that such affairs often ended in imprisonment without a fine.

While travelling in a railway train in Jersey a young garrison soldier, named William Hazel, Ist East Surrey Regiment, attempted to pluck a flower growing on the side at a hank. He overbalanced fell from' the train, and was dragged along for some yards. When picked up he was dead.

had a strong biasin favour of her own sex, and always gave a woman the benefit of the doubt._

Tlie -second problem was what should (she do? Should she tell Daisy that a warrant was out for her arrest and advise her to give herself up to the law? _ .

Miss Miggs had very little respect for the workings of the law—a law-abiding person herself, she yet looked upon the law as an engine that crushed the innocent sometimes if the guilty could not be found. Besides, Daisy by her flight had laid herself open to the deepest /suspicion, and Miss Miggs determined in her-own mind to let the law look after itself ari'd to help her new acquaintance to efface herself till at all events' the truth otthe matter grew more apparent. Arthur Esmond might make a full confes- sion, or the police might be unable to unravel the matter; time might do a lot, anti Miss Miggs felt in her soul that the more light there was cast on the affair the better it would be for Daisy. When she had determined on this, she said briskly : " Well, now that's settled. And the next point is where are you going to; you had much better come home with me: There is a room above mine, it's only five shillings a week, and it's to let, and I think, my dear, I can promise to get iyou some work in a day or two that will keep you going until we are able to look round us a little."

"Oh, how good you are," murmured the girl. "I believe heaven must have sent you to help me." , "I believe so, too," said Miss Miggs quite naturally, "but heaven doesn't help those who don't help themselves, so now let's be practical. Where's your bag ? " Daisy explained bow she bed left it at Paddington cloal4room and how she had given the ticket of] it that morning to the landlady in Portobello Road.

" I will go to Portobello Road," said Miss Miggs, " pay the landlady half a week's rent, and come back here; you can wait for me • 1 here."

Daisy consented, and Miss Miggs started off, catching a convenient 'bus that dropped her at the very corner of Portobello Road. Miss Miggs felt a bit nervous. Daisy was a remarkable-looking girl. Her description had been circulated in all the afternoon papers —bag and all; suppose for an instance this landlady had read the description,. or even the clerk at the cloak room at Paddington t Though London is such an enormous city there are maw ways in which a person may be traced, and traced swiftly, when they are " wanted."

Miss Miggs stood for a moment at the gate of No. Portobello Road. The house looked perfectly quiet, and the hall lamp was burning brightly behind the fanlight. Miss Miggs entered the gate, passed up the little walk, and knocked at the door.

It opened, disclosing the somewhat per- turbed face of the landlady. Miss Miggs also saw the brown gladstone bag on the hall chair, and there on the chair opposite the bag she beheld to her horror seated with note- book in hand the burly form of a policeman.

CHAPTER XIIL

INE left paisy Graham standing by the darkening water of the Serpentine. What might have happened at that moment it is impossible to say had not the girl's mind been attracted by a hand which was placed softly upon her arm. She turned and saw before her a little old woman who, from her appearance as she stood in the dusk, might have stepped out of a fairy tale.

This old lady.wore a bonnet of a somewhat antiquated cut, and she carried a cloth bag closed with a running string and stuffed ap- parently with angular objects from the bulg- ings and depressions visible on the cloth. She also carried a large umbrella. "My dear," said the strange old lady, "I see you are in trouble; can I do anything for you. Come, tell me." How much there is in the human voice ! How much it can express, as much as the human face, perhaps more. Daisy knew at once that the old woman before her was to be trusted, that she was a

friend sent to her by Heaven in her bitter need. " Excuse me, my dear, for my apparent rudeness," said the old lady, now agairr touch- ing Daisy's arm gently as if to lead her away from the water. " But you are very young and in trouble I can see, and anyone in trouble is my friend. Oh, I am well known," con- tinued the old lady, with a touch of pride in her voice, " and though I am poor people often come to me if for nothing else, for advice. Now don't trouble to tell me your affairs, dear, for I am not an inquisitive _ .

person, land everyone's affairs are their very own; you are troubled about something, that's enough; but let us come out of the park, it is getting quite dusk." "It is very good of you," murmured Daisy. "I am in trouble, great trouble; you may be able to advise me. You seem good and kind and I am sure Heaven has sent you, for a moment ago as I looked at that dark water I was on the point—oh! it's too terrible to 'think about." "Don't think about it, my child," said the old lady in a brisk voice; " don't think about it again. It is always darkest before dawn. Now let us talk about me, please I want to introduce myself; you have never seen me before perhaps ? " " No, ' said Daisy hesitatingly, "I don't think so." "Yet I am a public character in a way," said the old lady. "My name is Miggs. Have you never heard of Miss Miggs ? " "No, I'm afraid I haven't." _

"Ali well, how could you, young and be- longing evidently to the upper circles of life, how could you know anything of the wrongs of the people. lam a social reformer," said Miss Miggs; "for fifteen years I have devoted my life to the cause of the people, not only the English people, but the people of all nations. I write for several journals and I speak at public meetings, and I edit a magazine," con- tinued Miss IMiggs in a lowered voice, "which is forbidden to be imported into Russia. . The , Czar has his eye on me," continued the old lady in a voice that had a thrill of pride in it, "and if I were suddenly to find myself in St. Petersberg or Moscow goodness knows what might not happen to me. Never mind, here we are in the Bayswater Road; let us go this way. I want a cup of tealafter all my italking, and we can talk over our affairs as we drink it. There is nothing like tea, my dear, to give one ideas, and I know a little restaurant where the tea is tea; at most restaurants," concluded !the old lady, emphatically, "the tea is ditch- water." They walked towards Bishops' Road, Miss Miggs chattering away as if she and Daisy were old acquaintances,- the true spirit of tlit. woman showing itself in the way she managed to keep the girl's mind engaged and away from whatever trouble it-was brooding upon, and tc keep the conversation going without asking questions or seeming to invite any confidence, Miss Miggs at this part of her career was in- deed, as she had said, a social reformer pos. sessed of a little income sufficient for her wants and a great soul, which, if divided up, would have furnished souls for ' a dozen \ordinary people and to spare; she lived in two rooms over a shop in Museum Street, Bloomsbury, dividing her time between the British Museum library and hUmanity. She was undoubtedly a woman of consider- able brains, taking a deep interest in that greatest drama of earth—the life of the 'people around her. She read everything in the newspapers from the doings of kings to the police reports, taking most interest in the latter, and many a poor wretch had reason to be thankful to Miss Miggs for a home saved from ruin or a child saved from shame, for though Poor herself she had many friends who were rich and only too willing to help her in her philanthropic schemes. They entered a little restaurant in Bishops' Road,a quiet place quite deserted at dial hour, and Daisy's new friend called for tea and rolls, and fresh butter, placed her bag b,osh-le her, took off her gloves, and then said, "Now, my dear, we are comfortable and we can have a good long talk." " I had better first tell you my name," said Daisy ; " I wish to be perfectly frank with you. My name is Graham, Daisy Graham. I have lived all my life with an aunt of mine in Boundary Road, St. John's ‘Wood. Why, how pale you have grown. Are you faint ? "No, dear," said Miss Miggs with a gasp, "it is only a little heart affection I sliffei from. lam all right now. Go on please, you said you lived with an aunt." "Yes," said Daisy, "with ray aunt, Miss Princeps, at the Laurels, Boundary Road." The girl went on to tell of her life, how from the earliest times she had never known a kind word, how on the preceding evening a great trouble had happened to her, and how in a fit of desperation, not caring what occurred to her, she had left her aunt's house never to return, taking with her the clothes she stood up in, a few things in a bag, and' less than two pounds in money. Miss Miggs listened attentively till Daisy had finished, then she said : "I would like to meet your aunt. dear." "Oh, no," cried Daisy, fearing of a sudden that this new found friend might be going to play her false and deliver her up to the person she most dreaded. " Oh, yes," said Miss Miggs, "1 would like to meet her, just for the pleasure of tearing her bonnet off." Daisy laughed. "Not being able to do that," continued the old lady, "let us talk of other things. You spoke of a great trouble. Now, my dear, I am an old woman, but I know a great deal of human life. Forkive me for asking the ques- . tion, was this a love trouble ? " Daisy blushed. "Don't say a word," said Miss Miggs, " I have long been used to reading the human Countenance, and now, dear, for a moment just let me think."

She fell into a reverie. She knew now who Daisy was. She had read in the afternoon papers the whole account of the Bank Robbery, with the details. Arthur Esmond had been taken before the 13ow Street magistrate : Miss Princeps, re- covered from her fainting fit, had been led an unwilling witness as to the flight of Daisy; everything looked very black against the pair. Daisy's flight [absolutely clinching the matter the 'magistrate, on the evidence, having issued a warrant for Daisy's arrest. ENow two, problems. were resolving them- selves in Miss Miggs' brain—the first was, who was the guilty party? She felt sure !it !was Arthur Esmond, and that if Daisy had anything to do with the affair she had been dragged into it by this designing man. Miss Miggs, without being a man-hater.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XIVJ

A PERSON less remarkable than Miss Miggs would ihave betrayed herself by an excla- mation or a start, but Miss Miggs neither exclaimed nor started,

She had come to fetch the gladstone bag standing before her on the chair, yet she did not look at the bag or seem to recognise it. "Good evening," she said to .the landlady; "have you a room to let ? " "Will you come in, please," answered the landlady in a bothered tone of voice. She showed her visitor into the parlour and closed the door.

"I have a room to let," said she, speaking In a muted voice as if making some shameful admission. ".1 let it to-day to a young lady, but she's been and got into trouble.' "Dear me I "said Miss Miggs, " how sad!" "She's been and got into trouble," pursued the landlady; whose eyes were watering and -whose breath gave evidence of the presence of peppermint and gin. • "Robbed a bank; dear me! Such a perfect lady as she was, too; she left her bag with the cloak room clerk at Paddington Station, and he must have spotted her from her description. I sent my boy Billy to fetch the bag and back Billy came with the bag and followed by a detec- tive.

" Then they put a policeman in to watch. and there he sits in the hall a-shaming the house. When the poor thing comes back here she'll be caught like a rabbit in a trap." Miss Miggs listened to all/ this, feeling as if she were standing on the edge of a precipice. Then she said:

I am very sorry for your annoyance, it must be a great trouble to you, I'm sure; shall I return in the morning ? ' "Oh," said the landlady, " now you're here you may as well look at the room, it's swep' and aired and dusted like a new pin, and I nearly as ever was dropped when the detec- tive told me who I had swep' and aired and dusted it for. There's a kind of onnatural fate about that room ; first I let it to an Indian gentleman and he went off one day and quer came back, leaving a trunkful of brickliand my bill unpaid. Then an old gentleman took it who was eccentric, and I'd sooner have two lunatics than one eccentric in the house; then such a nice young gent took it; he was a teetotaller the first week, and then he burst out and was in deleriam tremens two days before they took him to the hospital, then a lady took it who upped and died within two months, and now this young lady took it and filled my house with police and detectives, Drat the room, it'll break my heart before I've done with it, that I believe.' She led the way upstairs to the offending bedroom. Miss Miggs examined it, feeling for the first time in her life a hypocrite. " It's very nice," she said, saki the terms for such a room seem reasonable, but my in- come is strictly limited and I would like a day to consider the matter. I will call to-mcrrow evening and tell you my decision; I daresay you won't have let it before then." The landlady assented and showed Miss Miggs downstairs and out at the hall door.

Outside in Portobello Road Miss Miggs drew a deep breath of relief; she had had a Marvellous escape and had saved Daisy by her coolness and daring. If she had wavered or faltered or shown signs of fear suspicion might have been raised and everything then would have been lost.

When she returned to the restaurant she found baisy just as she had left her, seated at the table.

jewi it

"How long you have been," said the girl with a smile, I" I thought you were lost; did you get my bag ?" • My dear," said the, old lady, "I think we had better give your bag up as a bad job, for the present, I went to the house you men- tioned. The bag was there, but—well, I'm afraid your aunt has traced you there and is cr. the watch."

Daisy clasped her hands. " How unfortu- nate,•what shall. Ido without my bag? All my night things, my comb and brush--" I will see what I can do for you," said Miss Miggs. " You will just come home with me and put yourself under my wing for a week till we see how matters turn up, and to-morrow I hope to be able to put you in the way of making mon,ey so that you will be quite inde- pendent after awhile as every young woman ought to be.. Come dear, I have paid for the tea."

- Miss Princeps had been carried into the little waiting room where clients were shown if the manager was engaged, and was recover- ing herself with the aid of salts and a glass of cold water.

•• Arthur, half stunned by the position he found himself in, could scarcely think. Daisy had run away I She had refused to tell the meaning, of that unlucky paragraph in her letter. Was there ever such a complication of bad luck? Had Fate ever hit a man so hard before ? With the instinct of a lover he knew at least part of the reason why Daisy had taken this fatal step—she was disgusted with him for having shown her letter. Yet how could he have done otherwise? Mr. Row- lands had practically seized it with the other contents of his desk. •If he had not given it up it would have been handed over to Scotland Yard. Yet—yet when one thought of it, a girl's first love letter, a thing absolutely sacred by all the laws of love and honour to be re- turned to her by the hands of a bank manager with brutal enquiries as to the meaning of part of it. No, it was a thing no woman could ever forgive.

ihese thoughts reduced his mind to sifch a state of despair and hopelessness that he scarcely noticed when the gentleman from Scotland Yard, after a whispered conver- sation with Mr. Rowlands and Lord de Morley, touched him on the shoulder and said, " Come with me, please." . _

He knew he Wis a prisoner. Well, what of that? He had lost Daisy, he had lost his position, he had lost everything; the loss of liberty was only one loss more. As he drove to .the Mansion House—for it was determined that the charge should be gone into without delay—the.people in the street seemed like the spectres in a vision and the sound of the traffic like the sound in a dream. , - Now, it will be remembered that Mr. Mar- cas, after his interview with the Grafter, was left in the bar parlour of " The Feathers" with the black bag, which the Grafter had stolen, to get rid of. The sight of the great wealth which the Grafter had so calmly. appropriated had acted on Mr. Marcas' mind as the sight of unlimited raw meat would act on the mind of a starving tiger. The great opal especially had fascinated him. Mr. Marcp was of Oriental•extraction ; he had the Oriental's love for beauty, and more than the driental's love for riches. Riches and beauty combined as they were in this exquiiite gem were entirely too much for him.

There is, and the history of crime proves it, a fatal fascination for the mind of man in precious stones. If one could write the his- tory of all the great diamonds extant it would form a terrible book. :^IZIPP3

• Now the opal with the strange inscription on the back of its plain gold setting, the giant opal that the Grafter had calmly pocketed, and the vision of which still shone before the eyes of Mr. Marcas, had a history which we will hear of later on, a terrible history from which it would seem that disaster had always come to its possessor or holder. Mr. Marcas knew nothing of this history, but he sincerely wished every imaginable disaster to fall upon the devoted head of the Grafter. He determined in his own mind to be even with that gentleman yet, and plainly speak- ing, to rob the robber if he died in the attempt. He finished the brandy in his glass, felt that the ten sovereigns were safe in his pocket, and then turned his attention to the bag. To a man like Mr. Marcas that was easily :disposed of. He took an old newspaper from his pocket, wrapped the bag in it, and with. this innocent looking parcel under his arm he left the public-house.

He too-lc a first-class ticket for Queen's Road, Baysivater, at the Notting Hill Station of the Underground Railway and at Queen's Road, Bayswater, he got out, leaving the bag under the seat of the carriage. He then went to a, restaurant and dined for the first time in several days. He bought several of the evening news- papers, but there was no word in them of any tragedy having happened in Holland Park. Next morning he was up betimes. He kit( that if a city clerk had been robbed and - dered the most likely place to get first wino the affair would be the Mansion House yO' Court. He arrived there at tie opening the court. , On. his way be had bought seVeral of the. morning papers, but still there was no word of the tragedy. The police, court was crowded with the ordinary cases, people who. had come to ask the magistrate's advice on all sorts of questions, women seeking separation orders from their husbands who had given them black eyes, husbands seeking separation orders from their wives, petty thieves, drunkards, all the unhappy people that the police sweep into their net and dish up each morning before the magistrates of London. -Mr.! Marcas watched the proceedings with impatience. At half-past eleven, however. be had his reward. Inspector BradstreEt, of Scotland Yard, entered the court and had a few words in private with the alderman on the bench.

Aievi moc ,nd poli of

Immediately after Mr. Rowrinds, Lord de Morley. and a veiled lady, who was none other than Miss Princeps, entered and took their places in the well of the court. . _

The alderman concluded the case he was hearing, the case of a costermonger who had refused to move on wheri told to do so by the police. . . . _

When it was finished Arthur Esmond was brought in and placed in the dock. A silence fell upon the court at the sight of the hand- some and well-dressed young man, pale, and standing like one in a dream in the place where thieves and forgers usually stood. ' The ease was- shortly explained to the alder- man by Inspector Bradstreet, and then the evidence was taken. In an ordinary case the evidence taken would only have been a.; much as was necessary to justify a remand, but since Daisy Graham was implicated and it was necessary, if possible, to obtain a -warrant for her arrest, the case had .to be gone into more fully.

Mr. Rowlands told his story, and Lord de Morley testified to the fact that the valuables lost were his.

As he gave his evidence he was watched by the lynx-like eyes of Mr. Marcas. " Now what," thought Mr. Marcas, "is. the meaning of this ? " Not only Mr. Marcas, but a number of the people in court, noticed that Lord de Morley was in an extreme state of agitation; his hand shook, he fumbled with his eye glass, he halted and hesitated; never did a witness show more trepidation than this noble lord testifying to the value of his own property. " If ever I saw a criminal," thought . the sagacious Mr. Marcas, " I see one now before me. There's money to be made out of this if I only see my way. Let me watch." " There were papers in the bag besides this .ellery ? " asked the alderman blandly. • Tirre were papers," answered his lord- ship. _ - • it iN ft

One man flung himself into the saildle, Jardine lid the same, two leaped into the wagon, and, as with a quickening beat of hoofs and whirr of wheels the cavalcade swept recklessly down the trail, Marston's roar broke through the mocking cries of the rest and the ranchers' cheers: "Ride for your life, Harry. Good luck to you!" For a space friend and foe rode level, muzzle to muzzle, and tail to tail, dropping the jolting waggon behind; then Jardine driving his beast at a screen of bracken, vanished among the pines, leaving his rivals bewildered. "I figured he'd have made a better race in- it," I said one. 4 It was a steep slope to the river, mattes with salmon-berry, slippery with shale but he went down it at a gallop, swaying low frio4he saddl. I to clear odd branches drooping between the great o ilumnar trunks. Then he was out on the shingle under the moon with the flocnl roaring past him towards the pool, and the snorting beast wcnt in with a plunge, as he drove the red spurs , home. Jardine, who cleared his feet from the stirrups, slipped from the saddle when the battering hoofs lost their grip on stone, loosed the bridle, and twined one hand in the mane, shifted it to the saddle, and saw nothing but frothing ridges while he trusted the brute's instinct to take it safe across. Whether he swam or was merely towed he was never certain, but at least the water supported .him, and the horse, which was used to shallower rivers, managed the steering, though now and then when &fey swung together across a smoother ' eddy he coula See the dark pines sliding very ' quickly up stream, and knew the big whirlpool , lay ominously crose below. But most of the time froth and water beat into his eyes, and the water was cold with the deathly chill of the glaciers • So at last it was with a gasp of fervent thankfulness that he heard sliding shingle rattle beneath the hoofs, and, dropping his own feet, he gripped the bridle and floundered shorewards waist-deep in water. He was in the saddle next moment, crashing at headlong gallop through the harsh swamp grasses towards the forest, while, when he swept into a narrow, tunnel-like trail, a half-seen man dragged two horses clear of it, and a voice cried: "Well done, well done! Don't spare the beast, Jardine." As he rushed past like a whirlwind a slender, white-robed figure waved a hand to him, and the rider's chilled blood stirred within him, for it was Tourmaine's voice which encouraged him, and he spared neither the beast nor himself. All trails are .bad .in that region, but the one in question led over a hog-backed spur which no mounted man could pass in broad daylight, while every minute was precious. Jardine had calculated that he could just catch the pacific express and reach Yale at least before his rivals mule •the long horse Sack journey 'to another mining recorder's station. They had taken it for granted no man could swim the river. At last he dismounted, and, so he afterwards slid, pulled the horse up almost vertically a mile, and once, \lien he tried to shove it, was badly kicked for lk a pains. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Buckley-street P.S.A. At the meeting of the Buckley-street P.S.A. Society on Sunday afternoon the half-yearly distribution of prizes took place. The meeting was presided over by Mr. H. Wooley, who gave an address on" Let us run the race set before us." Mr. A. Greenough proved himself a capable soloist in rendering "The children's home" and The Holy City." At the close of the meeting Mr. A. Jennings distributed about 200 prizes to the members. Mr. J. Urmston read the Scripture lesson and Mr. W. Shaw also took part in the service.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. There was a fair attendance at this meeting on Sunday, Mr. G. Smiley presiding. Mr. J. Billam, of Manchester, was the speaker. He made reference to the work of the Church,P.SlA. and the man. The duty of each was to undertake tHe work lying nearest to them' and to preach the great truisms of the past—the great gospel of peace, joy and salvation. They must not be discouraged by small apparent results, but be anxious about the doing of their duty, leaving the results with God. Mr. C. H. Mills also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. J. Barnes gave a recitation, also a vocal solo. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the piano. The Secretary announced than LI us. Bd. had been received for the organ that day, making a total contribution by the P.S.A., up-to-date of 420, towards defraying the cost of organ renovation. Six new members received a very cordial and hearty welcome.

Welcome to the Rev. E. J. Gawne. A hearty welcome was extended to the Rev. E. J. Gawne, the newly-appointed minister on the Warring- ton Wesleyan Circuit, at a meeting held at Penketh on Saturday evening. There was a large attendance, includ- ing. friends from Sankey Bridges. Mr. Withinshew, as senior circuit steward, took the chair. After the open- ing hymn and prayer, Mr. Withinshaw called upon Mr. Mellor, of Padgate, to speak, and Mr. Mellor in a very humorous manner welcomed Mr. Gawne, and told him he had come to a land of plenty—plenty of work,plenty of sympathy and plenty of co-operation. He also des- cribed how the circuit was planned, and how Penketh, Sankey Bridges and Moore were the districts allotted to Mr. Gawne's care. Mr. Manning then gave a few words of welcome followed by Mr. Ambler, a Penketh society steward, and Mr. Wadsworth, Sankey Bridges, society steward. Mr. C. E. Parker then addressed his welcome, to which Mr. Gawne replied, and referred to his previous five years of ministry. He thanked all present for the kind way in which he had been received. Refreshments were then distributed and social chat indulged in.' Songs and pianoforte solos were rendered and the gathering was in every way a success. The rev. gentleman preached at Bold-street Wesleyon Church on Sunday morning before a large congregation, his dis- course creating a very favourable impression.

Hill Cliffe Baptist Church. Services in celebration of the autumnal anniversary in connection with the above Church were held on Sun- day, the preacher for the day being the Rev. Daniel Hughes, of Liverpool. In the morning the rev, gentle- man gave an instructive•address,to the young people, of whom there was a fair number present with friends. The subject was "Moths," which he dealt with in a scientific manner and deduced many spiritual truths. He based his arguments on the words of Hosea: "Therefore, will Ibe unto Ephraim as a moth." The weather being favourable there was a large attendance in the afternoon,when the Rev. Daniel Hughes preached from another passage in Hosea " Sow to yourselves in righteousness, reap in mercy." The choir gave an anthem entitled "0 love the Lord." Tea was provided in the schoolroom for friends from a distance, and a goodly number attended. In the evening the chapel was crowded, the Rev. Daniel Hughes again preaching and taking as his theme the vision of Christ in the Book of Revelation: "His eyes were as a flame of fire and on his head were many crowns." This is the first time that Mr. Hughes has visited Hill Cliffe, and all felt that the great things said of him as a preacher were amply justified. Miss Brundett, of Lymtn; sang a sacred solo very sweetly, and the choir rendered the anthem "Holiest, breathe an evening blessing." Mr. Gandy presided at the organ, and the choir was under the leadership of Mr. G. Moss. The collections amounted to nearly £B. .

FOOD POISONS AND CANCER. -- Some Startling Results of Recent

Cancer Research.

Much valuable light has recently been thrown upon the nature and causes of that fearful scourge, cancer. Medical experts all over the world, helped and encouraged by the large bequests left by Nobel and other philanthropists for that purpose, are straining every nerve to discover the secret of this fearful disease, its causes, its nature, and its cute. Meanwhile the ravages of this disease are enormously increasing. According to the latest annual returns, the number of deaths certified as due to cancer and morbid growths of a canCerous natute were over 27,500 in Great Britain I alone.

Concerning the cause of this dread disease no definite conclusion has yet been arrived at, but it is acknow- ledged that cancer lies dormant in the bodies of many, waiting the opportunity for development which a low state of health alone provides. According to Dr Robert Bell, " the condition of body favourable to the development of cancer is due chiefly to vitiation of the blood, and the prime factor in this is the overtaxing of the digesting and assimilating organs. Much of the food eaten is not digested, but decomposed, forming poisons in the bowels, and polluting the blood. Constipation too, favours cancer development, as there is much absorption of fetid matter into the blood." Such facts provide still another powerful argument in favour of the adoption of a safe, pure and natural household medicine, Bile Beans for Biliousness. Unfor- tunately, mankind knows no cure, as yet, for such a disease as cancer ; but it is a distinct gain to know that by keeping the organs of digestion and nutrition in natural health we May successfully resist attacks. Bile Beans for Biliousness will keep your stomach, liver, and bowels in sound working order by virtue of their kotent herbal propertieS. They contain no hjtmful mineral drugs, and no drastic purgative, but they gently. and genially alter the internal disorder of your bodies, bringing health and vigour to weakened frames. This is the reason why Bile Beans are so uniformly success- ful in the cure of so many different ailments ; they set right the natural working of the stomach, liver, and bowels, and Nature does the rest.

STOCKTON HEATH.

ST.THOM AS''SUNDAY SCHOOL.—A number of teachers with friends, had a picnic to Lake Windermere on Saturday. Owing to the interest of Mr. R. Townley (London and North-Western Railway, Warrington) the party bad an extra 'large and comfortable saloon placed at their disposal. Mr. E. Weston (manager of the Furness Abbey Hotel) specially sent ovE r his assistant manager (Mr. J. W. Ashton) to supervise the luncheon the party had at the Lakeside Pavilion. The tour by steam yacht to Ambleside and Bowness, and the visit to Stock Ghyll Force were enjoyed try all. The party returned from Windermere at 6.40 after having a good tea at the Waterhead Hotel, Acibleside. Owing to the assistance rendered by Mr. W. J. Bell (Great Central Railway,Barrow),formerly, of Warrington,the party had many privileges accorded them. The Vicar (the Rev. James Collins) generously provided light refreshments both on the outward and return journeys.

Paraffin Lamp Tragedy.,

A paraffin lamp fatally acc-urred -at Warrington on Saturday, the victim being Mrs. Annie Ashworth Hindley, aged 68, wife of Stephen Andrew Hindley, a retired glass-maker, residing at 84, Greeuall's-avenue. Mrs. Hindley was retiring to bed shortly after mid- night on Friday, and was carrying upstairs a jug of water in her left hand, and a lighted paraffin lamp in iher right. When she got to the fourth step—so she informed Mrs. Blease,l26, Greenall's avenue, who saw her afterwards—the socket in the lamp slipped, and she upset it. Her. clothes became ignited, and her husband, who had gone to bed at ten o'clock, got up and found her lying at the bottom of the stairs. With an overcoat he put out the flames and then called in Dr. Collins, who attended to her injuries and ordered her removal to the Infirmary. She was conveyed to the institution in a cab,but she was so terribly burned iall over the body that she died at 12.15 the same day. i—At the inquest held by Mr. S: Brighouse on Tuesday IA verdict of " Accidental death " was returned.

THE EXAMINER.

SEPTEMBER 15th 1906.

Even the lay mind has THE LATCHKEY been much struck by the VOTER. far-reaching effects of " case " law. The decision of the House of Lords in the Scottish Churches case necessitated fresh legislation, the Taff Vale decision is the direct cause of the Trade Disputes Bill now before Parliament the recent judgment of the Curt of Appeal in the West Riding case decided in effect that Parliament had failed to accomplish what it meant to do in 1902, and now last year's decision in the Devonport latchkey case is providing work, and occasionally amusement, for those who are engaged in and about the Revision Courts. One effect of the deci- sion of the Court of Appeal is already apparent. Thousands of persons who have never been on the register before will, as a direct result, now become enfranchised, and many who have been on the list of Parliamentary voters only as lodgers will in future be entitled to vote as occupiers, which means that they will be able to Ivote at municipal as well as Parliamentary elec- tions. As interpretations of the law itself vary, so also, apparently, do interpretations of the judg- ments in such cases as Kent v. Fittall, judgments which are intended to lay down what the law real means. afferent revising barristers are givii different rulings, and it seems extremely lil, that we may have another test case in the - Court to elucidate the judgment, or, to be decide what is " control." One barrister rules that where the landlord resides on the premises the presumption is that he has control; another says that unless distinct evidenee of the control of the landlord is put before him he shall presume there is none. Which of these is the proper pro- cedure it is not for us to say, but we are always in favout of making access to the register as easy as possible. The Devonport decision was welcomed for the very reason that it meant the addition of a great number of voters to the list, and we are sorry to see the possibility of the number being limited by different-interpretations of the meaning of "control.' There is something distinctly humorous in the idea of a tenitnt having to call his landlord, who lives on the same premises, to say that he has no control. In one case the tenant cmtended that he con- trolled the landlord. There was a mutual arrangement that the last in at night should bolt the door, but the tenant persisted before the Court that while he had a right to demand that the landlord should bolt the door, the landlord had no right to demand that the tenant should do it. Therefore the tenant controlled the landlord. And so on. Whimsical situations are being set up all round, and revising barristers are having a very happy time. All this must, of course,. convince us of the labsolute necessity of some amendment of the IRegistration laws. What does it matter whether a man lives in a small house or is a ;joint tenant, or rents rodms from a land- lord who lives on the same premises? Under whichever condition he lives he is equally a member of the State, a citizen, and should , be given the rights and privileges of his position. ISo long as our present method of registration lasts so long will parties fight one another in the Revi- sion Courts, and will respectively endeavour to find reasons for keeping men off the register. Drastic reform of the Registration laws is needed. A shorter qualifying period is a necessity,and we do not see any reason why a man whose name is not on the register should not be able to make a sworn declaration before the Rsturnin,g Officer even on the day of election. The great anxiety displayed by the agents of both parties at this time of the year to keep people who are actually, but perhaps not technically, qualified is very saddening, for we are always sorry to see any citizen deprived o f the franchise. He is never deprived, on technical grounds, of the " privilege" of paying rates and taxes, and it should be made easy for every rate and tax payer to have his name on the voting-list, and to keep it there so long as he resides in the constituency. Registration reform will no doubt soon be tackled by the Government. SJ far as we are concerned, the sooner they tackle it the I better.

Was passed "in the absence of practical Proposals " from the Colonies. While the resolu- tion is interesting, as indicating .the desire Which exists for preferential tariffs within the Empire, it has no political .importance. The issue of Free Trade and Protection is dead ; nothing snort of a miracle can revive it. The tarift propaganda has been quietly dropped by its advocates in the Press. Free Traders have grown tired of pointing the moral of the trade statistics, Which month by month give a more complete answer to Mr. Charnberlain's challenge. The facts have finally destroyed the plausibility of the tariff case. Mr. Chamberlain took his stand on the facts, and had the misfortune to commence his campaign just as the facts were about to tell most heavily against him. He took exports as his test, and exports have been his undoing.

The revision of the voters' lists has not caused much trouble in Warrington this veer. We had expected some sort of aftermath from the general election in the shape of active opposition by the political agents, but these gentlemen preserved the usual amiable front to one another. True,the Conserva- tive agent opposed the votes of a couple of Liberal ex-councillors and succeeded in getting one vote refused. But when the Liberal agent questioned the qualification of Mr.R. Pierpoint's late election agent matters were almost all square again. There I Would have been little interest in the revision but for the advent of Mr. S. Gough, who still has a Page all to himself at the end of the register. Mr. Gough desired to have his name removed from the list of persons convicted under the Corrupt Prac- tices Act and to be re-instated as a voter. The barrister gave Mr. Gough a very patient hearing llnd acknowledged that is case presented some Ilovel features, but the best he could do , for him was to state that his disqualification as a 1 voter was only for 5 years' dating of course from the date of his conviction in February 1904. Mr. Gough had only a few hours rejoicing in this semi- reprieve, however, for at :the evening sitting, of the' Court, the Barrister announced that under the particular section of the Act upon which Mr. Gough was convicted he was disqualified for seven years and not five. Mr. Gough's fate therefore seems irrevocably sealed until 1911. But he is such a stubborn individual that we should not be surprised at him bobbing no again with another ingenious excuse either for entering the Town :,. Council or exercising the right Co vote. i

THE VOTERS' LISTS.

The peculiarity of the Harvest Moon is that it rises for several days nearly at sunset and about the same time. This has some- thin,, to do with the autumnal equinox. Probably 11.3 has also to do with the curious fact that. when the crops are quite ready to he taken in, • Providence provided, almost invariably, a spell of fine weather. But if the farmer thinks that such Weather is going to last, and that he need net bestir himself, he is usually disappointed. The Tule is that when the corn is ripe the greatest economy in time must be observed. The corn must be cut, tied in sheaves, and placed upright in stooks. Foar sheaves usually go to a stook, end the idea of soldiers "piling arms," no doubt, was originally derived from the luethod of stooking the corn. The object, of 'course, is that the straw may be thoroughly tiried before it is carted away and stacked. If stacked when damp, by spontaneous combustion the rick may catch alight. Therefore, at the Paement the corn is quite dry it should be taken , Now,with the perversity peculiar to humanity, it frequently happens that the best time for the harvesting is app7oached on a Saturday night, and , the Sunday is gloriously hot. Nevertheless, . although according to Scripture, the ass was Pulled out of the pit on the Sabbath day, if a farmer dared to harvest on the Sunday he would be e sent to Coventry by his pious neighbours. So be refrains, and grumbles inwardly while he attends bivine worship. That evening the mist descends, ' and next morning a fine rain saturates everything. Possibly the entire crop is lost, No matter ; religious observance must be kept up, thus . exemplifying the ancient proverb that there is = many a slip betwixt the stook and the stack.

Hitherto they have been oPened in Committee, and the determination to admit the public by their representatives to so important a proceeding is a step in the right direction. We possess one of the best Plunge baths in the county, and yet it is entirely devoid of up-to-date appliances for increasine, the Nenjoyment of the swimmer. At one time the bath Possessed a spring-board and swinging-rings, but both these adjuncts have disappeared. Possibly there was Some danger in the use of the rings, but the spring- board should certainly have been retained. Then, too, why cannot the Warrington bathers have a water- chute? This is a very simple appliance which increases the joy of the plunge bath many-fold. There is Plenty of room and depth at the Warrington bath for such things, and the Committee should sea that they are a little more up-to-date. Then there would be, in all Probability, an increase instead of a decrease in bath zeceipts. .

Andther section of Sankey-street is being re-paved with wood blocks this week. We are now within measurable distance of being able to refute the charge of possessing the worst-paved main street in Lancashire, though Bridge-street and Ilorsemarket- street still remain close contestants for the undesirable distinction.

* * *

Interesting reminiscense; of the political campaign- ing days of the late Judge Bowen Rowlands are given by the London correspondent of the " Manchester Guardian." He says :—Judge Bowen Rowlands had been out of politics for ten years or so, and he never made any great mark in the House. It should, how- ever, be remembered that he was the hero of one of the most interesting political fights in Wales that the present generation has witnessed. In ISM the late Mr. Davitl Davies, of Llandinam, grandfather of the present member for Montgomeryshire, represented what Wordsworth calls the " pleasant shire" of Cardigan in the House of Commons. By his own energy and abilities Davies Davies had built up a fine fortune. His friends said that he put the bread and cheese into the months of a considerable number of his constituents. He was also a warm and generous supporter of the great Calvinistic Methodist denomina- tion. Cardimmshire, of course, was strongly Protestant and Nonconformist. David Davies voted against the Home Rule Bill, and Mr. Bowen Rowlands came forward to oppose him as a Gladstonian Liberal. The contest seemed a hopeless one for Mr. Rowlands. He had not even the advantage of being a Noncon- formist or a wealthy man. He had been a curate in the Established Church before he went to the Bar. His powers as a speaker were also limited by the fact that he did not speak Welsh. All religious feeling was naturally enlisted on the side of his opponent. Still, in spite of all these difficulties, the Cardiganshire voters discerned the fact that Mr. Rowlands stood for genuine Liberal principles, and gave him, to the sur- prise of everyone in the constituency, a small majority over Mr. Davies. Never at any election did principle more completely triumph over interest and sentiment.

The unsecured liabilities included three for money lent £371 (including a liability to the bankrupt's wife £171) ; the rest were for trade goods supplied. , The bulk of the furniture was claimed by the debtor's wife,as a bequest from a dceased aunt. The remainder was claimed by the debtor's brother-in-law who purchased it from him in November last for £2O. The only book kept by debtor was a day book. He had not during the past three years at last prepared any statement of his assets and liabilities. He first became aware of his insolvency about ten years ago, and he had contracted debts since then in the hope that he would be able to discharge them out of future profits.— Questioned by Mr. Johnson, the assistant official receiver,debtor said he lost £3OO in bad debts contracted ' by workmen engaged in constructing the Ship Canal. His trade had decreased though the competition of the Co-operative Stores, which had been opened close to his premises, With regard to the licence, he had endeavoured to obtain this for eleven years, and he had spent considerably more than £lOO on solicitors, barristers and witnesses. He sold £3l worth of book debts for £ll about six months ago.—Mr. Johnson : With regard to the high wholesale prices. If the goods were sold to you at high prices why didn't you put on an additional sum ?—Debtor: The people would not I pav it, sir.—Do you think that is a justification for selling your goods at a sum that was not profitable ? It hardly is, sir.—Have you sold any of your goods at , a loss? No, sir.—The examination was then closed.

ST, HELENS.

THE MAYORALTY OF ST. HELENS.

Mr. Henry Martin will retire from the Mayoralty of St. Helens in November next, after having served in the office no fewer than four years. A statement has been circulated in the town that Mr. James Crooks, the chairman of the Gas Committee, had consented to become Mayor for the ensuing year, but Mr. Crdoks has definitely contradicted this.

SMOKE AND SMELLS. ST. HELENS HEALTH COMMITTEE AGAIN DISCUSS THE MATTER.

Ati{the meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee, on Wednesday, Sir David Gamble said he noticed that in some manufacturing towns the Health Committee did not allow the works' chimneys to emit black smoke for more than ten minutes in an hour. In some places the limit was seven minutes. In St. Helens, with their regulation of not more than five minutes at a time, it was possible for a chimney to observe the regulations and still emit black smoke for over forty minutes in the hour. He intended bringing the matter up when the report on smoke observations was submitted at the next meeting.—The Mayor (Mr. Martin) said that a great deal too much smoke was turned out. Something should be done to deal with the greater sinners, and something done to make the atmos- phere of St. Helens more pleasant. —The Chairman said that the inspectors only took observations when they had nothing else to do- If anyone was put specially on the work they would get as many cases in a day as were i now reported n two months.—Dr. Buchan said it took a great deal of time, for the inspectors had to make the observation in company, so that they could prove their case in open court if necessary.--Mr. Brown complained of a most sickening smell from the manure works.— After further discussion, the matter was referred to the Town Clerk and Medical Ofticer.—The Town Clerk said that all he could do was to prosecute.

A NOVEL ST. HELENS CLUB.

The Mayor (gr. H. Martin) presided at a special transfer sessions for St. Helens borough on Monday, when the other magistrates were Colonel W. W. Pilkington, Alderman J. Massey, and Messrs. J. A. Collins and J. Crooks. Chief-Inspector Goodall said he'had a report with respect to the Railway Hotel, Shaw-street, which had been submitted by Police Constable 70 (Evans), and which concerned Mr. Black- burn, the new tenant. The report was submitted on Sunday, August 19, and stated that the Constable had received information from a young man named Charles Murray, of Sutton, that he had joined a boozing" club at the Railway Hotel, . Shaw-street, on Friday night. Murray said they called there with a friend, and they both signed a book 'of some sort and became members. The conditions appeared to be that they paid ninepence fortnightly, which was spent in drink every Saturday night, and if they did not attend on a Saturday night they forfeited the ninepence. By the Chief Constable's orders, Detective-inspector Dunn made inquiries and saw Mr. Blackburn and the other man. The landlord gave him certain contribution cards and rules (produced), and said that if there was anything objectionable in the rules of the society he would have them amended. He said that the club was not a " boozing" club, but a money club which was in existence during the tenancy of the late licensee. The money was divided amongst the members annually.—Mr. Blackburn now said all he could say was that the club was in existence prior to his having taken over the premises, and he was simply continuing it. He saw nothing wrong in it at all. It was a dividing club.

Edward Litherland,- employed by a Warrington firm of brewers, was sum monad for having been asleep on his dray, and failing to have 'proper control of the horses.—P.C. Whalley testified to having seen the defendant asleep with the reins dragging on the floor, at Halton.—Defendant said he had been sitting up for the two nights before this occnrrence with his sick wife. He was very sorry.—A fine of 6d., with the costs, 9s. 6d. was imposed. PIGS ASTRAY.—George Cornelius Duckett was sum- moned for having allowed two pigs to stray on the highway on the 4th inst.—P.C. Barber said he found the pigs straying along the Belvedere on the date in ques- tion, and drove them to the defendant's yard. He there found a boy, who said someone must have left the gate open. Defendant had been served with a notice, pro- hibiting him from removing swine, on account of a suspected case of swine fever.—Superintendent Ennion said Duckett came to the station on the evening of the occurrence, and asked him to overlook the matter.— George Simmons (17) was called by the defendant, who contended that had only strayed to the yard gate. Simmons said they had got twenty years up the road. —P.C. Barber they were eighty yards away from the stables.—A fine of 125., including costs, was imposed. ASSAULT.—Thomas Riley, a Runcorn youth. WAS fined 10s. for having been drunk and disorderly in Church- street, on Sunday.—P.C. Wailing proved the case, and said prisoner twice struck a man passing in the street. A WORKERS' HOSPITAL.

It is stated that a well known Earlestown gentleman has offered on certain conditions to establish a hospital for the workers of the Vulcan Foundry, and to equip and maintain it with a suitable nursing and domestic staff, instruments and appliances.

LORD GERARD'S WEDDING. Lord Gerard's marriage with Miss Gosselin, which will take place in the Brompton Oratory on Monday afternoon, September 17, will be the great Roman Catholic marriage of the year.—The service is to be conducted by Father Bernard Vaughan, who, it is expected, will give a marriage address.

NEWTON CONSERVATIVE DEMONSTRATION. The second onnual Conservative demonstration held at Rainford Hall, St. Helens, on Saturday, was attended by about 1,206 Conservatives from all parts of the Newton Division. During the afternoon sports were held, and subsequently several speeches were delivered. 1 Colonel R. Pilkington, C. 8., welcomed the workers in the cause to Rainford, and went on to remark that the necessity for fighting was greater than ever it had been, because on the success of the Conservative and Unionist cause depended to a great extent the future prosperity, power, strength, and happiness of the king- dom. He did not claim that they had all the wisdom and patriotism on their side, for he believed that some Liberal statesmen and many Radicals had the cause of the country at heart ; but they would all agree that the I present Government had done nothing for the prestige, I the honour, prosperity, or security of the country. On the other hand, they were weakening the defences of the nation by their policy of the reduction in the Army and Navy. In regard to the Navy, it has been pointed out that, if the Government persisted in their policy, by the end of 1908 Germany and England would stand level in regard to navel strength—a posi- tion of affairs which would be very different from the standard set up by the Unionist Party. The Govern- ment policy was also to be condemned in regard to the Transvaal. We expended money and lives there in the necessary effort to uphold the honour of the Empire, , yet the Government, at the bidding of the extreme I Socialist tail in the House of Commons, now proposed ' practically to give back the Transvaal to the Dutch. 'ln this matter be charged the Cabinet with both I cowardice and treachery, and with acting in entire disregard of the interests of the United Kingdom. (Hear, hear and applause.) Miss Lamb, of the London Tariff Reform League appealed to the women of the Conservative Party in the Newton Division to assist in the organization of the party and to join the new Primrose League branch which was about to be formed. They could never have old-age pensions under a Free Trade system, because the country was already taxed up the lilt. The only way in which they could raise the £30,000,000 necessary for old-age pensions was by taxing the foreigner. She asserted, in contradiction to the statements they so often heard about protected countries, that there were no unemployed in Germany, for there they could not get enough labour to meet the wants of the industries. (Applause.)

BOY'S ATTFMPTED SUICIDE.

At the Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, before Mr. J. C. Parr (in the chair) and other magistrates, George Coulson, 16 years of age, was charged with having on the 29th August, at Stockton Heath attempted to commit suicide. by throwing himself in the Bridge- water Canal.—Frederick Hayes, of 17, Cawdor-street, Stockton Heath, stated that ke was a member of the firm of Hayes Bros., bakers, dru. The prisoner had been in their employ four months. On the 29th August they had occasion to speak to him about some offence he had committed. Witness's brother took him into the stable yard, and sent for the lad's mother, but in the meantime Coulson ran away and jumped into the canal. They got him out, when the prisoner asked, " Why didn't you let me drown? Let me drown myself." His mother took the lad home.—The father said his son was nearly 16 years of age. He was brought before that Court nearly two years ago for stealing. It was in a fit of temper that he threatened to throw himself in the canal. —Superintendent Ennion said that since that time the lad had threatened to throw himself through a window. —The father said that no later than Snuday his son threatened "to do away with himself," He had had him locked up in a bedroom for four days, and had taken his clothes away from him.—The Acting Clerk (Mr. F. G. Taylor) : You will not take any responsi- bility with reference to the boy, I suppose ?—The Father: No, sir; I would sooner leave him in your hands, so do what you think will be best for him.—The Chairman: And the mother, has she no control over him?—The Father: Not the least.—The Bench remanded the boy. for :1 week in custody, and during that time an inquiry will be made as to the condition of his mind.

Thirty passive resisters were before the Alarket Harborough magistrates on Tuesday. After consulta- tion, the Bench intimated that in view of the West Riding judgment they had decided to adjourn all the cases for six months.

The result of the principal event was a somewhat easy win for Warrington, their time'being 13 2-ssecs. against 17secs. The evolutions were witnessed with the greatest interest by a large crowd.

The Recent Cycle Parade.

- It is now possible to give a rough statement of the proceeds of the recent Cycle Parade on behalf of the Infirmary extension fund. The net sum raised apart from competitions was £220. The brick competition has realized about £77, and the watch competition £76, making a grand total of £373. The proceeds of the slate competition at present in progress have to be added to the above figure and will, it is expected, bring the total to about £4OO.

Warrington and District Shorthand Writers' Association. The opening meeting of the Session was held on 'Wednesday evening at the Winmarleigh Cafe from 7.30 to 9.45. There were a good number of ladies and gentlemen present, and during the first half of the evening dictation matter at various rates was given, this part of the Association's work thus furnishing an excellent opportunity for speed practice, a very important consideration to shorthand writers desirous of improving their phonographic capabilities. The latter portion of the evening was occuppied by the reading of a paper by Mr. A. N. Legge on "Charles Dickens." Such a popular subject was bound to be attractive, and the lecture was listened to with evident interest. A discussion upon the various points raised concluded a very pleasant and instructive night's work.

UNDER Trim CLOCK for Good Boom Advt.— Warrington Gentleman's Sad Death.

The death occurred at his residence Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, on Wednesday, of Mr. John Hartley Johnstone, under painful circumstances. The deceased who was 27 years of age, was a son of Mr. Johnstone who was formerly the principal of Lorne House School, Warrington. Ho was the managing clerk for Mr. William M. Johnston, Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, who carries on the business of ship broker and forwarding agent in Liverpool, and with whom he was shortly to have entered into partnership. About three weeks ago, when the two gentlemen returned from Liverpool they both complained of being unwell and both were confined to the house. It was at first thought that they were suffering from ptomaine poison- ing but it was later established beyond doubt that they had• contracted enteric fever, which in the case of Mr. Johnstone terminated fatally. He leaves a young widow and one child.—Mr. Johnston's life was also endangered but he is nowprogressing favourably. The cause of the sudden illness of the two gentlemen remains a mystery. -

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEET BOOTS DI LANCASHIRE.—AdVt. Alliance Box Co., Ltd. The armuel general meeting of the above company was held a few days ago, the Chairman of the Board of Directors, Mr. A. C. Russell (Messrs. Fletcher Russell and Co., Ltd.), presiding. The auditor's report and the balance sheet and accounts for the year ending June 30th were considered and approved, and it was resolved to pay a dividend at the rate of 5 per cent on the preference shares and of 21 per cent on the ordinary shares and to carry for- ward a sum of 309 Is. sd. to next year's accounts. The business of the Company continues to make satisfactory progress, and notwithstanding the fluctua- tions and changes in the requirements of certain extensive consumers the output of the company's work is constantly increasing. It was reported to the meet- ing that Mr. William Henry Bolton, a director of the Company, had died during May last while on a visit to New Zealand. Great regret was expressed at the sad intelligence and it was resolved to enter on the minutes a resolution to that effect. Mr. Bolton's place on the Board of Directors was left vacant for ithe present. Mr. P. Maginn was re-elected a director of the Company and Mr. J. R. Williams, chartered accountant, Sankey-street was re-elected to the position of auditor for the ensuing year. A vote of thanks to the chairman closed the proceedings.

LAWN TENNIS, CROQUET, and other outdoor sports.—J. S. GARLAND, 51,Bridge-st.—Advt. Opening of a New Baptist Church.

_ The new Baptist Church at Pontypridd, of which the Rev. Joseph Ellison (son of Councillor Ellison, of Warrington) is the pastor, was opened and dedicated on Thursday of last week, when Mrs. Ben Few, of Winwick-street, Warrington, sister to the pastor, publicly unlocked the door, in the presence of a large congregation. Before doing so, she delivered a suit- able address, and read an miginal poem of sixty lines, written for the occasion by the pastor. Mrs. W. H. Ford, of Grappenhall-road, Latchford, accompanied her. The dedication service followed the opening ceremonies, with an address by Alderman T. J. , Hughes, of Bridgend, President of the Glamorgan and Carmarthen Baptist Association. In the evening Pastor Ellison delivered the dedication sermon, from the words" My house shall be called a house of prayer Ifor all nations " Mark 11. 17. The edifice is galleried on three walls, and will seat 000 people. There is also a chapel or vestry that will seat 250 adults. It is built of stone with white brick facings, and has fifteen memorial stones in. the front wall. With the com- pletion of the work has come to pastor and people a splendid opportunity for the best endeavour in Christian worship and work.

ODDFELLOWS PRIZE DISTRIBUTI9N.

_— Progress of the Prince of Wales Lodge.

The members of the Prince of Wales LAge of Oddfellows held a pleasant social gathering at the head- quarters, Pelican Hotel, on Thursday evening, when the prizes won in connection with the fourth annual bowl- ing main were distributed. Bro. T. P. Bradshaw, Prov. G.M., presided over a good attendance, and at the outset apologised for the absence of Bro. George Crosfield.

The CHAIRMAN, in presenting the prizes, said the 1 bowling handicap was organised for the purpose of 'bringing the members and officers of the Lodgetogether as the position of a Lodge of the dimensions of the Prince of Wales Lodge could not be maintained unless they had the hearty and thorough co-operation of all its members. They had now a membership of 470. Unfortunately during the past half year they had not made thatiprogress which they anticipated but if all the members worked hard they would have no difficulty in achieving their object and attaining a membership of 500. (Applause.) In conclusion Bro. Bradshaw appealed to the members to take a greater interest in the Lodge business and to all strive to become officers not only of the Lodge but of the District. (Applause.) The prizes were then presented as follows :-1, T. Bolton (cup and medal presented by Mr. A. H. Crostield M.P.) ; 2, R. Cook ; 3, A. Shaw ; 4, R. Smith • 5, A. Cooper; 6, A. Whitman : 7, 11. Glover ; 8, E. Pinch ; 9, J. M. Finch ; 10, H. Maddock; 11, F. Wisedale ; 12' R. H. Holford : 13, j. Edelsten ; 11, D. Bromley; 15, H. Corless ; 16, H. Haslara. An interesting feature of the proceedings was the presentation of a framed emblem of merit to P.G. Bro. King for services rendered to the Lodge. Bro. HOLFORD, Prov. D.G.M. in making the presenta- tion spoke highly of the work which Bro. King had done on behalf of the Lodge. Bro. King had in words of the emblem endeavoured to his utmost to promote the welfare of the Lodge and of its members, and the mem- bers of the Lodge made the presentation as an aaknow- leolgment of their appreciation and esteem for the manner in which he had done his duty. (Applause.) Bro. KING- suitably returned thanks. His work for the Lodge had been a labour of love. He had always endeavoured to earn the esteem of the members and he was pleased to think that hehad succeeded. Bro. King I returned thanks for the assistance which he had always received from the officers and the members of the Lodge. (Applause.) During the evening an enjoyable musical programme was submitted, songs being given by Messrs.W.Dennett, E. Walker, J. Willett, T. Reddish, and duets by Messrs. Willett and Walker. Messrs. A. Pemberton and J. H. Foster acted as accompanists.
