'CROWN LANDS FOR SMALL

HOLDERS.

(BY JOHN TAYLOR.)

"More than four thousand acres of Crown lands are now vacant for want of suitable tenants, and Lord Carrington intends to offer these at once to small holders."—" Daily Chronicle."

I Earl Carrington, the new President of the Board of , Agriculture, is one of the best known land reformers in , England. He owns large estates in Lincolnshire, and Norfolk, and for the last twenty years or more he has carried out some interesting and successful experiments with allotments and small holdings in both counties. His private secretary, Mr. Richard Winfrey, M.P. for South West Norfolk, was the originator of the" Lin- colnshire Small Holdings Association," which was one of the pioneers of the movement for settling agricul- tural labourers on the land, and enabling them to live by their own efforts, and be their own masters. These enterprises have been so successful that many labourers who started at first with only one acre allotments, are now farming forty or fifty acres of land, and their farms are well stocked with cat tle,and pigs, and poultry and crops that they have earned and paid for by their own efforts. _ _

When these two able, experienced, and enthusiastic land reformers took up the question of offering Crown land for small holders, they had to spend a good deal of time in gathering information, and overhauling the methods and plans of the officials who have the control of these estates.

The path of every honest reformer is usually a bed of roses with the thorns uppermost, and it is always to somebody's interest to have no reform at all. So every possible stumbling block is put in his way, and he is opposed and thwarted and misrepresented at every turn.

1.-IT IS NOT EASY TO GET CONTROL OF THE CROWN LANDS.

You must first of all be able to manage those who manage them, and that is no easy matter. Ministries rise and fall, and Parliamentary representatives come land go, but the permanent officials in Government 1 Offices are there all the time, and they usually get their own way in the end. The Commissioners of Woods and Forests have not only the management of such Royal estates as the New Forest, and Windsor Park, and Dean and Here Forests, Ibut they have the control of 70,750 acres of agricultural Crown land. Most of this land is let to tenants, and it is widely scattered over our agricultural counties. There are 17,422 acres of this land in Yorkshire. Lincoln comes next with 13,092. Wiltshire is third with 8,804. Bedfordshire, Essex, Kent, Oxford, and Sussex have between three and four thousand acres each. Cheshire has 2,597 acres, and the smaller estates are scattered over Berkshire, Buckingham, Cambridge, Dorset, Gloucester, Huntingdon, Lancashire, North- ampton, Nottingham and Suffolk. More than four thousand acres of these Crown lands , are now vacant, and yielding no rent. The Isle of Sheppey, in Kent, has 1,157 acres lying idle, though the farms are within fdrty miles of London, and close to the mouth of the Thames. The Isle of Wight has 790 acres of good land that is not earning a penny or doing the nation any useful service. Suffolk has 572 acres, and Oxford 490, and Lincolnshire 357 acres of these i lands, unlet and unproductive. 1 These Crown lands were originally the property of i the King, and he received all the rents and revenues I from them. In 1760 George 111. surrendered them to the nation in consideration of an addition to his allow- ance on the Civil List. If he had stuck to them, and managed them well, our King Edward would probably have 'sheen richer than he is to-day. Some people I grumble at the vast amount of money he gets from the nation, but if they set against that the money the nation ' might get from those Crown lands, then we should have the cheapest king in the world. The Commissioners of Woods and Forests have managed these estates ever since they fell into the nation's hands, and they have given themselves as little trouble as they could. They have established a custom of letting large farms, that could only be managed by rich men who had plenty of money. To such tenants they granted long leases on easy terms, and they had little to do but receive the rents, and keep the accounts, and hand over the receipts to the Treasury. If a lease expired, and they could not find a new tenant rich enough to take up the farm, they did not worry about it, for the nation would never feel the loss of the rent.

You can imagine that an office like this, that has had its own way for nearly a century and a half, will not take kindly to new ideas, or submit quietly to anybody's control.

Lord Carrington tried to persuade the Commissioners to break up a few of their large farms that had no tenants, into small holdings, and let them at fair rents to agricultural labourers and small farmers, but they positively refused. So it required a special Act of Parliament to enable him to become master in his own office.

It is well known that the King heartily approves of small holdings and takes a keen and lively interest in this movement. The Prime Minister is also h. warm supporter of it, and his Cabinet gave Lord Carrington a pledge that he should have his own way in this matter The New Bill,which is practically through Parliament now, appoints the President of the Board of Agriculture for the time being as one of the Commissioners of Woods and Forests, and thus gives him a direct voice and vote in their management. It also sanctions the principles he advocates, and enables him to carry out his programme of reform. As soon as it becomes the law of the land a forward movement will be made without dr lay. Meanwhile, Earl Carrington and Mr. Winfrey have not been idle. It took a good deal of time to gather the information they required, and to start the machinery of their new reform. They could not antici- pate the Act of Parliament, or try to force the unwilling Commissioners to carry out a policy of which they did not approve, but they could kive them a practical illustration of the work thsy wanted them to do, and show them how to do it.

11.--TilE CROWN LANDS CAN BE PROFITABLY USED AS SMALL HOLDINGS.

Of course much depends on the quality of the soil, and more depends on the character of the cultivator. You must not wed these two together, till you are quite satisfied that both of them are fit, and suitable, and are likely to be successful. At the village of Burwell, in Cambridgeshire, there is a large Crown land farm of 903 acres, which has been without tenant for three years because the Commis- sioners said "they could not find a suitable tenant." If you pressed them for a definition of that phrase you would probably find that they wanted some substantial applicant who could guarantee the prompt and punctual payment of the rent. Now in order to keep this farm alive, and prevent the land from becoming utterly worthless and derelict, the Commissioners employed a farm bailiff and labourers, land stocked and worked the farm, probably at a loss. Earl Carrington and Mr. Winfrey satisfied themselves that they could make it pay if it were broken up into Ismail holdings, and let to farm labourers, who knew how to use it, at such rents as neighbouring farms would yield. So they resolved to try the experiment las a private venture and as a useful object lesson. Mr. C. D. Rose, M.P. for the Newmarket Division of Cambridgeshire,volunteered to accept all responsibility ' for the payment of the rent, and to become the actual tenant till an association had been formed, and was strong enough to manage its own affairs. Mr. Winfrey sent his agent down to Burwell to talk matters over with the agricultural labourers and explain his scheme of breaking up the farm and letting it in allotments and small holdings, and in less than a fortnight the whole of the land was applied for by labourers and small holders in the immediate neigh- bourhood. These applicants have formed a little com- pany of their own, called the "Cambridge Small Hold- ings Association," and before long they hope to relieve Mr. Rose of his responsibilities, and take over the entire management of the farm themselves. There are two farmhouses, and excellent barns and stables, and several superior cottages, and these will be allotted to the various holders. The plots of land will be of various sizes, ranging from two acres to twenty, and workmen are busy now cutting up the estate, and fixing the boundaries and erecting fences, and getting ready for immediate occupation. This scheme alone will settle scores of agricultural labourers and their families on that land for life. When they have got their houses in order, and their land under cultivation, it will take a good deal to tempt them away, and for every holder who dies or leaves the dis- trict, there will be a score of applicants for the vacant I land. Mr. Winfrey tells me that his Lincolnshire Small Holdings Association has already let 700 acres of land, and it ruts 2,000 separate applications for more land on its books.

Similar results have followed Earl Carrington's experiments in Norfolk, and his tenants there would gladly take up three times as much land as they have got if they had the chance of it. He has adopted a novel system of settling disputes among his holders about rents. If a man complains that his rent is too high, he summons a jury of cultivators,and asks them to examine

the land, and say by their verdict what the rent ought to be, and whatever they decide he accepts at once. It is his proud boast that the verdict seldom goes against him, and more than once the labourers have decided that the land was worth more than it was paying, sad the rent has been increased. You do not meet with a large landowner of this type every day. The farmers and landowners do not like these ailot- ments and small holdings, because they make the labourer independent of their offers of work, and they send up the price of labour all round. If a labourer gets three acres of land, and a few pigs and poultry, he will never work for anybody again for eleven shillings a week. He can make more by his own efforts, and he can be his own master into the bargain. This rn_qggment has now passed the experimental stage. It has laiproved to demonstration that capable men, on suitable land, can make small holdings an economic success. They can earn their own living, and brill..' up their families in comfort. But I value the mental, and moral, and social results much higher than money- making. When a man works for himself, and is his own master, and he is not everlastingly squabbling about an eight hours day, and a standard rate of wages, and trying to get more than he can earn out of somebody he does not even keep a sharp eye on the clock, for fear he should do a stroke too much.and he does not rush off to the public house, or the football field, to waste his time and money. If he has a bit of land that he can cultivate for his own profit, he spends his spare time there. He learns thrift, and sobriety, and forethought., and he makes himself into a good citizen, and wins his way to independence, and prosperity. The nation has nothing to fear from such enterprises as these, and we may all be devoutly thankful for the good work that is being done.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

(Continued front Page 3) Services for Sunday. Bold-street Wesleyan Church, preachers, 10.30 Rev. J. Manning; 6.30, Rev. E. J. Gawne. Bewaey-road. Wesleyan Church, preachers, 10.30 Rev. S. Pitt ; 6.30. Rev. W. Mellor. Wycliffe Church, preacher, 10.30 and 6.30, Rev. J. Yonge. _ . _

Wycliffe Re-opening. After having been closed some considerable time for re-decoration Wycliffe Congregational Church will be re-opened on the 23rd inst. At the services which will be held the Rev. John Yonge will be the preacher and special anthems will be given by the choir. Free Church Council.

A conference in connection with the South-West Lancashire Federation of Free Church Councils will be held at Warrington on Thursday, at which an address will be delivered by the Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, president of the National Council.

St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street. The congregation of St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street, are to be congratulated upon the handsome addition to their fine Church in the shape of the new tower which is now almost completed. The structure is a most graceful and imposing one and is erected in the Gothic style in harmony with the rest of the building. A peal of bells is to be hung in the tower and a special dedica- tion service will he held early in October. There was an interesting little ceremony on Monday morning 1 when the top stones of the two final pinnacles were duly laid. At the invitation of the Rev. Father Wilson, the first stone, at the north-eastern corner, was laid by a well-known Warringtonian, who had taken considerable interest in the erection of the tower, regarding it as a distinct addition to the architectural beauties of the town, whilst the last at the south-eastern corner was laid by the Rev. Father Wilson. The top stones of the two western pinnacles were laid last week, the first by the Rev. Father Pritnavesi and the other by a tradesman who resides in the immediate vicinity.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
St. James', Latchford.—Re-opening Services.

For some time St. James' Church, Latchford, has been undergoing renovation and re-decoration. The 'work has just been completed and on Thursday evening a large congregation assembled at the re-opening service. The decorations have been most tastefully and artistically executed by Messrs. H. and S. Chandley and the improvement in the appearance of the church is very striking. A very pleasing feature, which adds much to the brightness of the buildinrz, is the intro- duction of new hangings in the sanctuary. Thursday , evening's service was of an appropriately festal ' character and included the anthem "Magnify His name" which was very creditably sung by the choir. Mr. Davies presided at the organ. The preacher was the Lord Bishop of Chester (Right Rev. Dr. Jayne) who took for his text the words : "And whatsoever ye do, do it heartily, as unto the Lord, and not unto men ; knowing that of the Lord ye shall receive the reward of the inheritance ; for, ye serve the Lord Christ " (Colossians iii., 23-21). He pointed out that these exhilarating and lofty words were spoken to slaves.. How did the Apostles of Christ go to work on the terrible evil of slavery which they found everywhere? They did not proclaim a crusade against it—that would have done untold mischief to the cause of the slaves. Instead they set to work to undermine the institution of slavery by transforming, in the power of the Spirit of Christ, both the slave owner and the slave himself. It was noteworthy what lofty and exhilarating adomini- tions the Apostles, and especially St. Paul, addressed to slaves. Continuing, the Bishop pointed oat that if St. Paul could address such words to the slaves of that time how much more vere they incumbent upon the Christians of to-day who were not held down by bondage.He exhort- ed his hearers to live always as in the sight of God,to begin by tilling the garden of their own souls, and then to work for God's Church, for their country, and for the neighbourhood in which they lived, making the lives of others brighter, fresher and sweeter. He concluded by congratulating the congregation upon the work which had been done by a multitude of willing workers and urged them to go forward in the path of loyal co-opera- tion. He was glad that something had been done, even in these doubtful and distressing days for those who loved their Church schools and the best interests of the education of the people, to improve the excellent school which the parish possessed. Then they had paid off the debt on St. Hilda's Mission and put the building in improved order. The chief work, however, had been the renovation of the parish church and they had only to look round to see how beautiful and helpful, through the eye, the church had been made. He trusted they would continue to work together harmoniously for if i there was any work in which there should be harmony lit was that which was done for God and the Church.

PRETTY WEDDING AT CAIRO STREET CHAPEL.

Pemberton—Madeley.

; There MCA a very pretty wedding at Cairo-street Chapel on Wednesday afternoon,the contracting parties being Miss Dorothy Benton Madeley, daughter of Mr. , Charles Madelencttrator of the Warrington Municipal Museum, and Mr. Robert Pemberton, son of Mrs. Pemberton' 13elveir House, Whitefield-road, Stockton r Heath. Considerable interest was taken in the ceremony, the bride and bridegroom being well- ; known in the town,and a large congregation assembled. The Rev. C. Harvey Cook, minister at Cairo- street, officiated. Mr. J. M. Muir presided at the organ and played appropriate selections, includ- ing the "Bridal March " from " I.ohengrin " and Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." The Wride was charmingly attired in a white embroidered Swiss muslin robe, with white hat and feather, and carried a lovely bouquet of white roses and foliage. She was attended by two bridesmaids—Miss Emily Pemberton. sister of the bridegroom, and Miss Mary Hill—who wore pretty dresses of flowered voile, and white hats. Mr.'Benton Madeley discharged the duties of best man. At the close of the ceremony,a reception was held in the Congregational parlour at Cairo-street ehapel,the guests including, in addition to members of the two families, Mr. Monks, Mrs. Holme (Upton Grey), Mr. H. Stone, Miss Stone, Mrs. C. N. Milner, Mr. and Mrs. C. Pemberton, Mrs. A. S. Wainwright, Mr. and Mrs. H. Bond, Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Woodcock, Mr. and Miss Shaw, Mr. and Mrs. James Ward, Rev. C. H. and Mrs. Cook, Miss Perris, Mr. J. M. Muir, Mr. and Mrs. T. P. Rathbone, Mr. and Mrs. J. S. Hill. Later in the day the newly-married couple left for North 'Wales ; where the honeymoon is being spent. The. bride's ; travelling dress was a green cloth coat and skirt, with hat to match. Numerous handsome presents were received.

Town Clock in Danger.

The town clock was placed in considerable jeopardy i on Thursday evening, but fortunately t escaped with- out sustaining any serious damage. .While passing along Sankey-street some persons noticed flames in the clock tower, and a large crowd rapidly collected. The fire brigade was summoned, and was quickly on the scene with the steam engine " Major " and the reel cart. The services of the engine were, however not required, as the fire was. quickly extinguished with a single jet. Upon examination it was discovered that a large beam had become ignited, though the cause was not ascertained. The damage is slight.

TRAM ACCIDENTS ANALYSED.

An amilssls of accidents on several tramway under- takings shows that the proportion of reported accidents of all kinds is about tone accident to every 15,000 passengers. Among these are :—Collisions with horses, vehicles, and cyclists, 502 per cent. ; falls on entering or leaving cars, 273 ; breakdown of vehicles on track, 6.1 ; knocked down by passing vehicles while entering or leaving cars, 35 ; accidents to employes, 2.4.

RAILWAY, COLLISION NEAR WARRINGTON.

Guard's Narrow Escape.

A somewhat serious collision occurred On the London and North-Western Railway at Winwick Junction, near Warrington, shortly after five o'clock on Tuesday morn- ing. A fruit train, on its way from Crewe to Wigan, was standing on the line waiting for a signal to proceed when a parcels" special" from London to Carlisle came along at a good speed and crashed into the rear of the fruit train. The brakesman's van was completely wrecked and seven waggons immediately in front were thrown off the rails and more or less damaged. Brakesman Heath,of Crewe,who was in the van,bada wonderful escape,suffer- ing nothing more serious than a severe shaking and a slight scalp wound.He was ableto walk to a goods train which was passing, and in this he was conveyed to Warrington and medically attended to by Dr. Braide. He afterwards proceeded to his home at Crewe. Immediately after the accident the news was tele- graphed to Warrington, and in a short time Mr. Bolton, stationmaster at Bank Quay; Mr. C,resswell, manager of the locomotive department, Warrington; and Mr. Parsons, of Liverpool, who occupies a similar position, were on the scene with breakdown gangs. Before noon the waggons had been replaced on the metals. All the waggons with the exception of a fruit van, which was battered in at the rear and left on a siding, were taken away in their damaged state. The driver and fireman of the parcels train were much shaken, but were able to continue their journey. The only damage done to their train was to the buffers of the engine, caused by the impact. As the line is a double one, traffic was practically uninterfered with by the accident.

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of this society was held at the Old Academy on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. F. V. L. Mathias. The attendance included : Mrs. R. Lowe,Mrs. A. C. Smithson, Messrs. J. Harding, sen., P. Dalton, W. Winstanley,A. Davies, J. W. Brown, F. Garside (hon. treasurer), and A. C. Smithson (hon. secretary.) A SATISFACTORY YEAR.

In the course of his annual report the HON. SECRE- TARY (Mr. Smithson) remarked that auy drawbacks or disappointments of the past year had been quite counter- balanced, and he was enabled to chronicle what had been, on the whole, a fairly satisfactory year. The membership of the Society now stood at ninety. The committee felt that their efforts for the welfare of the Society and the furtherance of tho object of the Society's existence, which was the advancement of the art and practice of photography, warranted them in appealing for the support of the members generally in bringing the advantages of membership to the notice of new and old practitioners. The old as well as the new hand had , always something to learn, and the latter would not fail to receive that assistance through lectures,practical demonstrations, &c., which was necessary to success in pictorial photography. In all. about 121 msetings had been held during the last winter session and not the least enjoyable of these were tlae demonstrations given by senior members for the benefit of beginners. Mr. D. S.Birrell began the series and Messrs. Bishop,Bodman, Moore, Cartwright, Harold Smith, G. Smith, F. V. L. Mathias and J. S. Hill also contributed instructive, practical demonstrations. More advanced lectures were given by Messrs. C. J. Harrison, H. Wade and J. Page Croft. It was to be regretted that a large number of members did not avail themselves of these special lectures. Their affiliation with the R.P.S. enabled them to obtain lantern lectures on flower photography, &c. The summer outings might be written down as a failure, owing no doubt to people desiring to go away as opportunity offered on their own account. Another method of arranging outings was necessary. They were members of the Lancashire and Cheshire Union of Photographic Societies,and with the aid of the Secretaries' Postal Society they were enabled to inspect prints by good workers all over the country. The exhibition was held from May 17th to 25th again at the Art Gallery, by the courtesy of the Museum Committee, and in this respect the society desired to record their appreciation of the services of Mr. Madeley and the Museum officials, who certainly did all they could on behalf of the exhibition. (Applause.) The collection of pictorial photographs and lantern slides was amongst the best that had ever been got together in the North of England, exhibits being received from nearly all parts of the British Isles,as well as from the Continent. The 142 exhibitors sent in 365 pictures, 200 lantern slides and 102 pictorial post cards. The Japanese tea room was a popular feature, and their thanks were due to Mrs. Mathias and the other ladies responsible for the success of that department. The result "just about saved the situation," for although they had an increased revenue from exhibition fees, there was a decrease in .donations to the extent of £5 or £6 Considering the educational value of such a free exhibition to the general public' the lack of support was to be regretted. They rejoiced that two of the original promoters of the society—Mr. J. Harding and Mr. P. Dalton—were still enthusiastic members, and if they needed a reminder of their first president, the late Mr. T. J. Down, they had it in the support accorded the society by his son, Mr. A. Down. (Applause). Mr. K. F. Bishop was the winner of the president's competition last year, Mr. H. Bond running him very close with a picture afterwards exhibited at the Royal. Mr. Mathias had kindly repeated his offer this year. Several of the members had won distinction in outside competitions. Mr. J. V. Moore won the Warwick prize of £lO, which carried with it £5 for the Society, and Mrs. Smithson, Messrs. J. W. Mills, K. F. Bishop and J. B. Tolley had also scored. Mr. Mathias and Mr. H. Bond had had slides selected by the R.P.S. for circulation. They might be pardoned for assuming that membership in the society had been a most important factor in these successes. Great things were to take place during the ensuing session, including visits and lectures by Mr. W.J.Pearse, an artist of repute (member of the Society of Arts and examiner to the Technological Department, South Kensington) and Mr. F. G. Tryborn, winner of awards for pictorial lantern slides all over the world. (Applause.) The report was adopted. Mr. GARSIDE presented a summary of receipts and expenditure, which showed that the receipts were £2B Ts. Td. The year concluded with a balance owing the treasurer of 4s. 10d. The exhibition receipts amounted to 256 3s. 3d.and the expenditure to 253 7s. Ud.

The accounts were passed. Mr. SMITHSON regretted that the committee had not been supported so well in regard to the lectures whioh, he added, were worthy of the attention of every member of the society on account of the advantages they afforded. If the members did not pull themselves together the society would go to the wall. The CHAIRMAN remarked that it was fortunate they had the .£5 won by Mr. Moore; otherwise the accounts would have shown badly. Mr. HAROLD SMITH observed that several gentlemen had expressed surprise to him that their society and their exhibitions were so successful.

The CHAIRMAN said great credit was due to the Secretary for that. (Hear, hear). Several of last year's officials declined to again accept office. These included Mr. Mathias as president and Mr. Smithson as secretary. In regard to the latter position_the meeting failed to find a successor to Mr. Smithson and the election had accordingly to be adjourned. Other officials were elected as follows : President, Mr. F. G. Taylor; vice-presidents Mrs. Mathias, Alderman Bennett. Messrs. F. V. L. Mathias, P. Dalton, J. Fairhurst, E. J. Hall, J. Harding sen., and W. Winstanley •' treasurer, Mr. F. Garside ; lanternists, Messrs. 13.5. Owen and J. Tolley; auditor Mr. A. Pennington; curator, Mr. F. J. Eardley ; committee, Mrs. A. C. Smithson, Messrs. D. S. Birrell, K. F. Bishop, W. E. Brown, J. Dolan, F. W. Purse, H. Smith, and A. C. Smithson.

A NEW DEPARTURE.

Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons' Enterprise.

An entirely new departure--and one which has attracted considerable attention -has just been made by Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, the well-known Warrington firm of cabinet manufacturers and furnishers. Messrs. Garnett have long enjoyed a reputation for productions of the highest class but they have now determined to place themselves in line witti many other leading concerns by catering also for the middle-class artizan and others who wish to furnish tastefully yet inexpensively. With this object in view they have opened the premises next door to their present showrooms in Sankey-at. -formerly occupied by Messrs. Boyden. Bros.-which have been fitted up in the most pleasing and up-to-date style. The spacious show rooms are will stocked with a great variety of furniture and on examining the articles one cannot but be impressed with the excellence of the design and manufacture. The surprising feature is , the reasonableness of the prices, which are brought within reach of the moat limited means. The goods comprise handsome -dining room, drawing room and bedroom suites, tables, chairs, cabinets and many other useful and ornamental pieces of furniture. To give our readers some idea of the prices, it may be stated that one can buy a lounge chair in tapestry for 15s. 6d. All the goods are of choice and artistic description, and have been greatly admired by the large numbers of people who have been attracted thereby since the opening of the shop on September 5. The stock is not confined to furniture only, for the firm have made pro- vision to meet the requirements of customers in every , section of house equipment,and have a fine stock of oil- I cloths,linoleums,curtains and blinds. Our readers will be ! interested to hear that Messrs. Garnett have taken up ; the Lancashire agency for the " Gunn " sectional book ; case. This is a unique speciality -decidedly the best article of its kind on the market - the great advantages being that the case is absolutely dust proof, and that , it can be procured in section i just as the purchaser desires to extend or limit his resources.

Mr. Set;ll7—the courteous manager of the new department, will be pleased to conduct visitors through the show rooms at any time.

• Mr DELZ CHILDIANN, No doubt you are all anxiously waiting to hear the result of our Needlework Compe- tition. I am very pleased with all the tidies that I received, and I was a long time before I could settle upon the first prize-winner. How- ever, a prize has been sent to Lucy Fitzpatrick; I must say I think her work very beautifully stone,and I hope she will be pleased with the very nice book I sent. The second prize I had great pleasure in awarding to Olive Kingdon; the toilet tidy she sent is very novel, being made 'of plaited paper. This niece is very lucky, as ahe received a first prize not long ago; I hope she may win many more. The third prise goes to Mary M. Smythe. I shall be glad to hear from you all if the prizes have pleased you. I am very pleased to welcome Horace Inott as a member of our League, and hope he will send me contributions for "Our Corner."

The boys have sent me several suggestions for a competition for themselves, and I hope in a week or two to arrange something for them; as I have said before, I have some very nice prizes waiting to be won by our boys. No doubt you. have all returned to school again by this time. You must all study very hard, and try and gain prizes at the end of your scholastic year. I shall hope to have letters from you soon, telling me how you are getting on, and what you did during your midsummer vacations. _ _

I have sent a prize this week to Lizzie Wills for her very interesting history of "A Cat." Hoping to have many letters from you all and with all good wishes from UNCLE NED.

Dear Uncle 47ed,—This is the first time I have written to you. I was introduced to the "Children's Corner" by a friend of mine. I am twelve years of age. I have read the "Children's Corner" nearly every week, and I see that you sometimes give prizes for those who send you a piece of poetry. So I thought I would send you a piece. Please will you send me a free bottle of Mason's Extract of Herbs? From your niece, BERTHA LISTER. THE WIDOW'S LITTLE BOY.

Mother, come and kiss me, please, before you

go away. Oh, mother, when I'm all alone, how weary

seems the day. I count the hours, I listen hard, and then I

hear the chime Of all the city bells, ae one by one they eound

. _ the time. _ v Mother, do you think it very wrong of me to

fret? I wish I could be always good, but then, I soon forget; And when the pain comes back, although I do not wish to cry, I scarce .can keep the tears away, although I

- really try. Mother, don't you think it strange the things that people say? I've heard that some folk think that time is passing swift away. I'm sure that it can't be true, for when I'm lying lonely here, Each day that passes seems to me as though

it were a year. I fear lam not patient, aa a boy of seyen should

. . be, But how I love when Sunday comes, for then you stay with me I And .though I drop asleep, I know I'll wake,

, and find you here, And so I could not fret, although that day

- , should be a year. Mother, ..clo you think that I shall live to be

a man? For if I do, oh, mother dear, I'll help you

all I can! But if I die while I am small, still Jesus cares for mei, And, mother, when we are both in Heaven, how happy we shall be! THE TWINS. In form and feature, face and limb, I grew so like my brother, That folks got taking me for him, And each for one another. It puzzled all our kith and kin, It reached an awful pitch, For one of us was born a twin, Yet not a soul knows " which," When quite an infant child My troubles did begin, FOr when I called for nourishment,

It was given the other twin. They gave " me" Godfrey's Cordial, When he kicked up a shine, And when his nose was troublesome., They took to wiping mine. One day, to make the. matter worse, Before our names were fixed, As we were being washed by nurse, We got " completely mixed." And thus, you see, by Fate's decree, Or, rather, nurse's whim, My brother John got christened "me," Aid I got christened "him."

AN AUTOBIOGRAPHY OF A CAT. I shall be eleven years old iu October; yoa would scarcely believe it to see me..Withont being conceited, I will tell you that I am a handsome English Tom Cat, a beautifully marked Tabby. My mother was a Persian, and when I was a frisky playful little kitten, about ten weeks old, I was given to a dear old lady. This old lady named me "Micky." I quite well remember the day I left my mother and entered my new home, which consisted of the dear widow lady and her two daughters. They were. Very kind to me, and I love them all dearly. My mistress also had three canaries. They sang so sweetly, but alas they died after I had been with my mistress about two months. Their names were Dick, Sweet and Goldy. I was lost once about a year after my arrival in my present home. I was away for a fortnight, and only five doors away from our house I got shut up in a hen-house. Search was made for me round the square, and there was great joy when I was found.

Well, for seven years I spent a very happy, uneventful life. Then came the change, and I could not think what was the matter, till I heard my mistress say that we were leaving our house and going somewhere else. All the rooms were upset, and I was almost frightened out of my life by men taking. furniture away, and the house was quite emptied. I always hated cleaning down, but this was something more. I was put in a basket in the van and taken to our new home. This is a nicer houee, and I have a very nice private garden. The worst trouble I had I shall never forget. We were all going to the seaside. My mistress had been ill, and she was ordered to the seaside. The doctor said that if she, went, the two daughters must go too, and then it was arranged ' that I should also go. I was put into a basket, then into a railway-carriage. There was the most awful noise—whistling and screaming. I thought it would never cease. I was glad when we reached our destination. I could not make out the sea, or whatever they called it. I never saw such a lot of water. I liked to eee the children with their bare legs paddling in the sea. They did not seem to mind the waves one bit. There were also a number of dogs in the sea, after sticks. I was glad when I knew that preparations were being made for our return home, although I did not like the thoughts of the train ride. Well, we arrived safely home, and about a week after we had another addition to our family. This was a beautiful Persian kitten, called Sam. We have such fun together. I must now close my story.— MICKY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l hope my "Autobiography of a Cat" will interest you. I now close my letter, with love to you and my cousins. LIZZIE WILLS. Dear 'Uncle Ned,—l have read the "Children' Corner" a great many times, and thought it very interesting. I shall be very pleased if you will accept me as a member of your League. I am sending you a story, which I hope you will like. It is entitled: NICELY CAUGHT. In a hotel in London a man kept a tortoise in a basket. One night when the man had gone to bed, and all was in darkness, a man lifted up the window and got into the house. He felt in all the things, and at last he came to the basket where the tortoise was. He put his hand in and at once began to squeal, because the tortoise had hold of his finger. The man at once came down to see what was the matter. He found that it was a thief. He sent for the policeman, and he soon took him off to gaol., From your loving niece, EMMA ROBINSON.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a little piece of poetry, called, "A Word to Boys,' which I hope you will print, if you think it worth it. I like reading your " Corner " very much.' I am enjoying my holidays, and hope all my cousins are doing the same. I must close now, with best loves From your loving niece, MARGARET SABLE. A WORD TO BOYS.

Do you want some day to be great, boys?

You must first be good and true. Would you rise to high estate, boys? Then you mus'n't mind what you do. So long as it's honest and right, boys, You should do it, and do it well; You should do it as in God's sight; boys, For you know He can alwaYs tell! Stand up for the small and/Weak, boys, And help them whene'er you can; It is for this that we seek, boys, In a well-bred gentleman. Don't mind if your jacket be old, For a new one you'll get some day; 'Tis foolish to grumble and scold, boys, And it is not a pleasant way. Don't rail at the aged and poor, boys, For you may be old some day; If alms they ask at your door, boys, Don't drive them in wrath away. For their poverty's hard to bear, boys, You should cheer them whene'er you can; And let each of you have, a care, boys, That you act as a gentleman. And always stand up for the right, boys, Though you may stand with the few; For right is not always might, boys, Though it may be good and true. An this is the way to be great, boys; is a way that you all should heed; And the way to high estate, boys, Is by many a noble deed.

LIGHTLES 3 CYCLES.

In prosecuting two St. Helens masons, at the County Petty Sessions on Tuesday, for riding from Rainford without lights on their bicycles, Police-constable , Howell told the story of a somewhat serious bicycle I accident. One of the defendants appeared—namely John Farrell, of 87, Fry-street, Parr. and pleaded guilty, but Robert Halsall, of 70,Greenfield-road did not appear , The officer said he saw the defendants go backwards and ; forwards every day to work along this road. On Mon- day night the 27th ult., at half-past ten, they passed , him in pasture-lane, Rainford, riding without lights. , He shouted to them to stop but they went on. About ' twenty minutes later the officer was called into a house ,at Rainford-road, Windle, where he found the two ; defendants and a man named Beckett, who lived at Crawford village. It was said that when they were , passing Beckett, who had a light, there was a collision , between Farrell and Beckett. A doctor had to be called, end Beckett's injuries were so extensive that it needed eight stitches to put his head right —Farrell said they were made late by having stopped to see a bowling match.—A fine of is. ad. costs was imposed on each defendant.

PENKETH.

PREsENTATIoN TO MRS. CARMAY.—" St. Mary's Great Sankey Magazine" says :—On Sunday afternoon, at Penketh Mission, Mrs. Carman (nee Scott) was pre- sented with it handsome timepiece on the occasion of her marriage.—The vicar, on behalf of the teachers, requested her acceptance of the timepiece, and in doing so referred to the length of time Mrs. Carman had been identified with the Mission.—Mr. Hall also bore testi- mony to the goodwill existing among the teacheis, and their high regard for Mrs. Carman.—Mrs. Carman, in a few well-chosen words, thanked the teachers for their kindness, declaring that she would ever have pleasant recollections of her association with the Mission.

FIRE AT SANKEY STATION.—At two o'clock pn Sunday afternoon a waggon in the Sankey station goods yard was found to be on fire. It is assumed that some hot I , cinders from a passing train alighted on a sheet which covered a waggon containing two barrels of paraffin 1 ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. oil. Owing to the hot and dry state of the sheet it soon took tire, and in consequence came in contact with WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. ' the oil, and in the course of a few minutes the waggon I was blazing furiously. Mr. Newton. the stationmaster, ! FIRST DIVISION. was soon on the spot, and with the assistance of plate- , . P. W. L. D. For ,Agt. Pts. lavers, signalmen, and porters endeavoured to isolate Rylands the burning waggon. This was accomplished with some Castner Kellner difficulty and danger, owing to the large volumes of Warrington Albions smoke, livit, and flames. Whilst some of the men were , Thelwall 1 10 0 2 1 2, uncoupling the waggons and pushing them up the Palatine siding to a place of safety others were throwing soil, ' Latchford Christ Church sand, and water on the burning mass. Although the Orford Barracks men worked very hard, they were unable to save the Halton Villa lO 1 0 1 2 0 waggon, which was destroyed. RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S GAMES.

G. T. P. G. T. P *Broughton R. ... 1 3 11 Bradford *Oldham *Widnes *Bunslet 4 6 26 Dewsbury 2 1 7 *Keighley .:. 3 5 21 York *Leeds 6 5 27 Huddersfield 3 3 15 _ _ _

*Warrington Salford 5 6 28 Liverpool C. 0 1 3 *Kingston R. 1 5 17 Leigh 0 2 6 *St. Helens *Barrow Halifax 3 418 Wakefield T. 0 2 6 *Swinton ll 18 76 Pontefract Played on the ground of the club marked *

Warrington v. Hull. At Warrington, before 5,000 spectators. Warrington had wind and sun in their faoes during the first half, and immediately pressed, clever work by Hookenhall and Isherwood compelling Hull to adopt safety tactics. Taylor cleared, and Freear took up a passing movement initiated by Wallace, only to find Fish safe, and another return to the Hull quarters saw Taylor and Isherwood combine well, for Fish to put on the finishing try. Hull rallied well, and clever work by Wallace and Harrison led up to Jones dropping 9, goal. The game was very fast, and Warrington excelling in back play again pressed Taylor, who repeatedly cleared his lines in brilliant style. Following a fine round of passing IBrooks scored, and near the interval Naylor accepted a pass from Lees and got over. Half-time score :—War- rington, 1 goal, 3 tries (11 points),Hulll goal (2 points). Warrington again attacked on resuming, and after Taylor and Freear had checked Fish on the left the ball was sent swiftly across to Brooks who raced over near the posts, Preston adding the goal. Hockenhull broke away from a scrummage at half-way, and a single- handed effort resulted in another try. Taylor scored near the posts, Preston adding the goal. Hockenhall broke away and scored in brilliant fashion. Hull rallied well, and Harrison placing his side in an aggressiva position, Carvell scored, and also added the goal. Warrington pressed hard in the concluding stages,and Brooks added his third try. Result :—Warrington 3 goals, 9 tries (33 (points), Hull 2 goals, 1 try seven (points). Teams : Warrington—Berry, back; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Brooks, three-quarter backs; Lees and Hockenhall, half backs; Preston, Boardmau,Harmer, Heath, Naylor, and Thomas, forwards. Hull—Taylor, back; Freear, Harrison, Jones, and Jenkins, three-quarter backs ; Wallace and Burchell, half becks ; Major, Hargreaves, Shaw, Walker, Barton, and Carvell, forwards. Referee, .hr. F. Renton, Hunslet; touch judges,Mesurs. W. Mainwaring (Salford) and J. H. Cawley (Runcorn).

===== 0002601_19060915_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
OLDHAM v. WIGAN.

At Oldham, before 7,000 spectators. Play was fast and exciting in the first half, but owing to the heat slowed down considerably after the interval. The visitors attacked strongly at the outset, and Jenkins tried a drap at goal, which failed, when a try seemed easily obtsinable. Leytham then narrowly missed kicking a penalty goal twice. After this fortune turned to Oldham, and working up the field the home team obtained a footing in the Wigan half. Tyson got the ball and kicked across from the wing to centre, where Dixon taking the ball on the run, broke through all opposition and scored a capital try. Oldham still kept up the pressure, and Cash dodged over the line, White failing at a long kick at goal. Leytham ran well, and then kibking across to Brookes, the last-named crossed the line, and Leytham kicked a goal from the easy position. Before half-time White kicked two fpenalty goals for Oldham.—The second half opened tamely but ended in exciting fashion. Maclean 'a knee gave way, and though he rammed. he was of little use to his side. White kicked a penalty goal,and just before time Anderson scored for Wigan. Mason adding a goal. Scores :—Oldham 3 goals. 2 tries (12 points), Wigan 2 goals, 2 tries (10 points).

ST. HELENS v. ROCHDALE HORNETS. Played at St. Helens before a good attendance. Schofield started for Rochdale, play opening in their half. Devitt and Upton, respective wings of Rochdale, were within an ace of scoring in the first few minutes, but Atkinson kicked into the visitor's half, where Prescott g,lithered, and eluding Mallinson, scored under the post or Toole to place a goal. Rochdale then got dangerous, and Threlfall nearly got in for them, Atkinson tackling him. Toole, intercepting a return from Mallinson, dropped is goal for St. Helens, and immediately afterwards Briers got over, Toole kicking the goal. Rochdale got away, but the Saints forced them back, and Mooney got over for Toole to kick a goal. Wharton ran in under the posts for St. Helens, Toole again improving. Half-time score :—St. Helens, 5 goals 4 tries (22 points), Rochdale Hornets nil. On the restart Rochdale livened up, and eventually Devitt got over, but Mallinson failed to improve from an easy position. The Saints again asserted themselves, and passing between Prescott and Hillen resulted in the latter crossing the line near the posts, Prescott con- verting. Rochdale then penned St. Helens iti close quarters, and from a splendid bout of passing, in which all the backs participated, Schofield got over at the corner, the place kick failing. „Result :—St. Helens 6 goals 5 tries (27 points), Rochdale Hornets 2 tries (6 points.)

===== 0002601_19060915_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES v. RUNCORN.

At)Widnes, before 3,000 spectators. The visitors won the toss, and G..i.kspey started for Widnes against a stiff breeze and bright sun. After some scrambling play at centre, G. Aspey and Turner performed a grand dribbling movement, which resulted in the former scoring a good try. Taylor failed at goal. Harrison, the Runcorn full back, was Injured, and had to retire for a short period. Runcorn then had the best of the game, and just before the interval Deakin scored at the corner, Jolley failed at the goal kick. Half-time score: Widnes 1 try (3 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points). Immediately on restarting Runcorn were penalised close in, and Taylor had no difficulty in scoring a goal. Following upon this Runcorn appeared to be able to do nothing right, and the home lot had matters pretty well all their own way. Turner scored from a cross kick by Ditchfleld, and just before the finish the latter registered a third try. Result :—Widnes 1 goal 3 tries (11 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points)..

===== 0002601_19060915_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

Meeting of the General Committee.

A meeting of the General Committee of the Northern Rugby Union was held in Manchester, on Tuesday evening. E. Hesketh, a professional of the Runcorn Club,mtv'e an application for reinstatement as an amateur. The application was refused. M. Lambert was reported for signing professional forms on behalf of the Warrington and Salford St. Bartholmew's Clubs. Lambert was sus- pended for one week, and the Warrington Club exoner- ated from blame in the matter. It was decided that the following additional instruc- tions be given to referees in respect of cup ties : " That full stipulated time be played in all matches, and that any stoppage of the game from iniury or any other cause shall be noted by the referee, and the extra time played."

===== 0002601_19060915_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTHERN LEAGUE.

A meeting Of the above League was held in Manches- ter on Tuesday. On September 1, the match Oldham v. Runcorn at Oldham, started late, and the explanation was accepted. The following additional local fixture was confirmed : ' —October 27th, Runcorn v. Dewsbury ; January 12, Dewsbury v. Runcorn. It was decided by the committee to call the Special attention of club managers, and spectators to Rule 26 in the Official Guide Book, which reads :—"No attend- ance upon players shall be allowed on the field except in case of injury, when permisson may be given by the referee for such players removal from the field of play." The following local players were placed on the registers of the clubs first named :—Liverpool City : R. H. Findlow (Warrington), T. Cook (Warrington), S. Hopkins (Runcorn). Runcorn : T. baulkner and J. Jobes (local). Swinton: C. Garside (Warrington). Warrington : W. A. Landor, J. Kay. P. Boardman, and W. A. Barker (local). Widnes : A. Pennington (local).

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

_ Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19060915_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Morton and tin I.L.P.

Sir,—A few of our progressive leaders, particularly Mr. Winston Churchill, have appeared recently in their war paint to engage in a spirited conflict against the works of I. L. P-ers and I. L. P-isro,of whom and of which they have vowed to obliterate all traces. Still I think no' one is affected by the indecorous war whoops of the brilliant member for North West Manchester when we know it is impossible to put an old head on young shoulders. Mr. Morton enters the arena in a different guise—without either tomahawk or olive branch—and presents to our view an original method of extinguish- ing the Independent Labour men. He conveys to me the impression that he believes that the Liberal Party and the Independent Labour Party are heavenly twins and that the last-named has stolen the programme of the former. Of course this is not a crime bat:' reflects to its credit" and the only objection is that the little twin has been making itself too conspicuous of late, and it is now recommended to sub- merge its identity and practically commit suicide. If it absolutely refuses and still elects to exist " then the satisfaction remains of preventing it"taking ambush in our labour organization.' Might I ask Mr. Morton which is" our labour organization." "Trades unionism has built up its great strength by keeping party politics outside and in the interest of Labour generally it is essential that its vitality is not sapped by political Intrigues." so says Mr. Morton. Let us see. W. Abraham, Right Hon. T. Burt, C. Fetvvick and J. Wilson have, I believe, sat in the Commons since 1885. R. Bell and W. R.. Cremer sat later, whilst E. Edwards, F. Hall, J. Johnston, W. Johnston, F. Maddison, T. Richards, A. Richardson, W. C. Steadman, J. Wadsworth, J. Ward and J. Williams were returned at the last general election. All are life-long trade union officials. They have stood frequently on Liberal plat- forms, advocated the claims of Liberal capitalist candidates,and worked hand and glove with the Liberal party, fearlessly proclaiming themselves as Liberals. They were not returned to Parliament without incurring election expenses and when there they do not live on air. Are their election expenses and Parliamentary wages paid by Liberal millionaires or by the pence of Liberal, Conservative and Socialist workmen connected with theirfparticularltrade unions? Let us get an answer on this question and we can talk with greater freedom of " political intrigues." It is true that " items of the I.L.P. programme have been advocated by sections of the Liberal party long before the I.L.P. was born." Yes and it is also true that the intelligent Liberal and Conservative workman is sick of generations of false promises and broken pledges,and as Professor Beesley I said in the "Beehive."—"The working man has at length some to the conclusion that the difference between Liberal and Tory is pretty much that between upper and nether millstone. The quality of the two is essentially the same, and on all Parliamentary questions affecting the interests of labour they play into one another's hands so systematically and imperturbably that one would suppose that workmen never read a newspaper nor hear a speech."—Yours, etc. A CASWORKER.

The Right to Work Committee. Sir, —The fact of Mr. Morton in your issue of Saturday last, setting out to give the" public, and especially the working classes" a true conception of the constitution and the aims and objects of the Right to Work Com- mittee, occasioned no small amount of pleasure to myself, in-so-much that I thought Mr. Morton quite capable of carrying the task to a successful issue and declaring that the Committee had lost no time in getting to work in right earnest and although severely handi- capped mean to bring and are bringing the question of the "right to work" tr. the front. But alas! I was dis- appointed. The bitter thought of it. Mr. Morton, a leading light of the Progressires to be so far behind the times Now, Mr. Morton has, doubtless, been "on the earth" rather longer than myself, and as it has been part of my moral training to always respect my elders," I dare not presume to take to myself the role of dictator. Still I think r may safely Venture, in as respectful a manner as possible, to tell him that his statements so far as the Right to Work Committee is concerned, are not quite correct. First. then,let me tell Mr. Morton that the Trades and Labour Council have not "disappeared from the business." And secondly, that the Right to Work Com- mittee is not " disconnected from its base, floating I balloon-like." And further let me remind him and also the "public and especially the working class I section"—yon see we are still in sections—that the Right to Work Committee is not the Trades and Labour Council, nor the Labour Representation Committee, nor the Independent Labour Party. That is what it not. Now what is it? Simply this. The T. and L.C..L.R.C.,andl.L.P.each appoint five delegates and these fifteen delegates constitute the Right to Work Committee. That's the thing very briefly. Nor is this all. Mr. Morton says: "The Trades Unions themselves, after hoisting this new-fangled—note the term—Committee into the political atmosphere, dis- appear from the business leaving the Committee, discon- nected from its base, floating balloon-like etc." In that case the Trades Unions had better organise themselves into search parties and at once go in search of the Committee. Now why does Mr. Morton make such absurd statements as these? He must be totally ignor- ant of the Committee. The Trades Unions, through the Trades Council, still send their delgates and bear their portion of the financial burden. Further argument is needless. Now a word as to the course we are taking, and according to Mr. Morton, it is " a course quite at vari- ance with the democratic principles guiding true trade- unionism, and which seems to call in question the bona-fides of the Unions concerned." Now one would naturally think that the very name of the Committee was sufficient to convince the public what the Com- mittee staads for—the "Right to Work." The Com- mittee is anxious that every man, unionist and non- nniodist alike, shall have this right granted to him by the State. Personally, I think trade-unionists don't pat sufficient force behind this claim. Unemployment affects all classes of workers. The chances of getting better wages and all-round better conditions of labour are minimised to a considerable degree when there is such a large portion of the workers unemployed. What most concerns the workers during a strike, the masters or the blackleg? The answer is obvious. And what breeds blacklegs ? Unemployment. Let the trade- -unionist ask himself how long he could withstand the bitter cry of his children for bread. It is the children for whose exsitence he alone is responsible, and the woman he has vowed to support, that force him to sink his trade-unionism and take a job at any price. So when the Committee say they want to alter all this, they are calmly told it is contrary to the principles guid- ing true trade-unionism. When there's a strike what are the men then? Politics, nil ! The men are out on strike. They stand together. That is not contrary to trade-union principles. But when a similar course of action is taken by the Committee, when all political differences are set on one side, and the men are classed as unemployed, and the 'Committee are anxious -to voice their claims, " it's contrary to, etc." •If a man is unemployed he is nel•her Tory, • Liberal, nor Socialist. He's unemployed. And because .of that the Committee want to help him; not by ...doling out tea and bread and coal and blankets daring the winter, but by giving him work. Honest men ask for work—not charity, and the Committee say 'let them have it—that is, useful productive work. Let the unemployed have access to the land that is now lying idle. The resources of nature are boundless and God's creatures are starving ! And why? "It's a .course quite at variance with/true trade unionism " to ask for work. In conclusion, why does qr. Morton speak so of the Right to Work Committee ? Because the Committee did not say " Yes, send alone the Progressive Labour 'League, or in other words Messrs. Thomas Barnes, S. Wright, 3. Elwell, J. Morton, and Rev. H. Dunnico " the latter has been called away since then. And the veason we didn't was stated in a letter which I was instructed to write to Mr. Morton, which is "quite at varience, &c." But these men were to represent a body of -TOO. I wonder bow msnv of the 703 knew abont these delegates being appointed. With reference to the latter portion of Mr. Morton's letter I will just say one svord—`• Piffle I " and—smile. JAMES lISAIPSON, S 4 R.W.C. , Garibaldi-street.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist."l In Warrington the opening first team fixture under the altered conditions of the Northern Union has given satisfaction, and the officials of the club are, meta- phorically speaking, rubbing their hands in joyous anticipation of increased gates and a considerably enlarged bank balance as the result of the season's working—provided of course, that Warrington can maintain their present form. The thirteen, for their opening match at home, wete in their happiest mood and when in this vein the strongest combinations in the Union have to keep open their weather eye, and be prepared for squalls. To c3ntinue in nautical language, it was not a squall which broke over the devoted heads of the Third Porters, it was a perfect hurricane which struck them with a fury that they could not withstand and left them stranded a hopeless and complete wreck.

I Perhaps never in their history have Warrington had a league team so thoroughly at their mercy as were Hull. True some few years ago they ran up a bigger sore against Goole, but in that case nothing else was to be expected. Goole were an obscure and by no means strong combination, who had the temerity to challenge Warrington's right to progress in the cup ties, and they suffered accordingly. Hall, on the other hand, are a team with a reputation to maintain, and have earned for themselves the right to be classed amongst the first rugby teams in the North. One has a right to expect from them a game well up to the average standard, and this is exactly what Warring- tonians on Saturday did not get, for the simple reason that Hall were so hopelessly outclassed that they never once got properly going.

It only required a few minutes for the home team to demonstrate their superiority as, even though playing against the wind and sun, Fish capped a passing movement by placing a try to his credit. From this stage the home team settled down to show the true possibilities of the new rules in the direction of open and finished play, and the spectators were, by them treated to eighty minutes of interesting football. What added to the excitement, however, were the constant fluctuations in the game, and to illustrate this only one incident need be recorded. Hull had had a spell of defensive work, when they broke away and made a dangerous and determined rash to the Warrington line. No infringement of the wiles occurred when their progress was stopped and the Warrington forwards got to work and carried play to the hall-way line where they retired in favour of the backs. Still play was in progress and the ball travelled in the direction of Brooks who dashed over and grounded under the posts. Anything more entertaining than a movement of this kind even the most exacting rugby enthusiast could not wish for, and had Hull displayed a little better form such rapid changes would propably have been far more frequent than they were.

Hull entered the arena with a cheery self-confidence but quickly realised that in their endeavours to take two points away with them, they had, to use the expression of a man in the crowd, "bitten off lust a trifle more than they could chew." Beginning indiffer- ently their play degenerated as the game advanced, and never once did they look like doing anything in keeping with their reputation. The whole secret of their non-success was lack of speed. Whatever plans they had formulated for the taking and sacking of the Warrington fortress, they never had the slightest chance of putting them into execution. They were utterly demoralized by the tactics and speed of the home team until, heart-broken, they gave up all hopes of victory and strove only to keep down the Warrington score. Only once or twice when Warrington eased up a little did they get really dangerous, and on these occasions, with one exception, Warrington again roused themselves in time to prevent a score and to reverse the order of the play. Of the whole combina- tion only one man—Taylor—displayed anything like the true form of the Third Porters. It is hard at any time to catch the Hull full back napping and on Saturday he played a really magnificent game and time after time kept Warrington at bay when the other defenders had been overcome. Had his colleagues shown anything like the same form the result might have been different—hut that is in the region of hypothesis and we are considering facts.

It might appear that the above criticism of the visit- ing team would detract from the merit of Warring- , tou's performance but any such impression can at once be banished. Had Hull been at their best War- rington would have won. They were in that humour which declines to even think of defeat, and it is doubtful if any club in the whole of the union could have beaten them or even shared the spoils. In fact it was one of those rare—unfortunately rare—occasions when Warrington were at their best. Their tackling was vigorous and effective and their tactics worked with machine-like regularity. Would that they could maintain this standard throughout the season. They would carry everything before them ; clubs would fall easy victims to their powers and but again we draw upon the imagination and lose sight of facts, and what is only to be expected is that, like all other teams, they will bare their off days. Let us hope that these will be few and far between.

To come to the players, it is difficult amongst the half-dozen who stiod out pre-eminently to say who was the best, hut perhaps Brooks is entitled to claim that honour. In past days Brooks officiated behind he pack at a time when the Club Committee were crying aloud for a first-class right winger. By accident he was tried on the right wing, and it is only accident which will now occasion has removal—may the acci- dent never lay " our baby" low. Brooks has ever been regarded as on the slow side, but so far he has dis- played a surprising turn of speed, and promises to rival Fish as a prolific scorer. On Saturday he placed three tries to his credit —by no means a bad afternoon's work.

To dispose of the threequarters Taylor and Isherwood as centres were sound and reliable without being brilliant and kept their wings well supplied. On the left wing Fish, after opening in a manner in keeping with his reputation, fell off and for the greater part of the game declined to needlessly exert himself look- ing for work. At half Hockenhall showed a welcome return to form and his try was the tit-bit of the after- noon, being the outcome of a determined individual effort which carried him over the line after having traversed half the length of the field and passed through the Hull backs. Lees also was up to the mark and the committee need have no anxiety as to who should operate at the base of the scrum so long as he and his partner are on the effective list.

T 1 amm the forwards played up well and a harder working lot it would be difficult to place in the field. Preston, Thomas and Boardman were the pick and the first two in particular combined good dribbling with effective handling. At full back Berry was safe, and accomplished some exceedingly smart things. Unless there is a great falling off in his play —and of this there is little occasion for fear as he is one of the most con- sistent custodians—it will not be long before county honours come his way. Taken all round the score in Saturday's match fairly accurately represented the play and there is no doubt that Warrington were ahead of Hull to the fall extent suggested by the margin of points in their favour.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 15, 1906.

Warrington's engagement to-day is not one to give them cause to fear and tremble greatly, though they cannot expect any cheap points at St. Helens. Last season Warrington beat the Saints twice—by 16-0 at home and by 8-0 at St. Helens. They should repeat the performance this season, and on present form there should be no doubt of victory. St. Helens have so far had two home matches, their visitors being Swinton and RoChdale Hornets. The first they lost 6-10, and the other they won by 27-6. Apparently, therefore, their form is not very great. Still they have good forwards and are sure to make a bard struggle with their Warrington neighbours.

So far, no change has been required in the War- rington team, and the same successful thirteen are chosen to do duty again. The forwards will probably meet their match but in the rear division I am hoping to see Warrington manifest a superiority which will result in a substantial victory. The selected are as follows :

Berry; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, Brooks; Hockenhull Lees ; Preston. Boardman, Heath, Hamer, Thomas and Naylor.

By the way the form of Jack Fish has been the most disappointing feature of the football so far. He is showing none of the dash and brilliance which have won hem his reputation, and the club supporters are looking to a better exhibition to-day than on the two previous Saturdays. I believe, myself, that Jack can play as well as ever if he wants, and it's time he wanted !

The" A " team will be at home for the friendly fixture with St. Helens "A," who are not now membe rs of the Lancashire Combin‘tion. They should again record an easy win. Jordan has been selected at half-back in order to give Lawlor a trial on the wing. The team will be :

Knight: Harris, Cartwright, Dickinson, Landor ; Jack Davies, Jordan • Foster, E. Lunt, sea., E. Lunt, jun., P. Boardman, Barker, and Bradshaw.

T. Cook and R. F. Findlov have been ,eiranted their transfers to the Liverpool City club. Lambert has been suspended by the Northern Union COmmittee for a week, owing to his having signed professional forms for Warrington and Salford St. Bartholomew's.

At the conclusion of the play Captain Crosfield pre- sented the Cup and medals to the winners and medals to the runners-up. Afterwards the finalists, officials and friends sat down to a substantial tea at the invitation of Captain Crosfield.

Cycling. The Warrington Cycling Club bring their season to a close to-day (Saturday) and they have every reason te congratulate themselves upon having experienced one of the most successful seasons on record. Gener- ally speaking, the weather has been most favourable to the pastime and there have been large musters for the runs to various delightful rural haunts. To-day the final run will take place to Sankey and the bowling handicap which has aroused so much interest will be concluded. Afterwards there will be tea, followed by a concert.

Last Saturday's run was to Helsby but owing to counter-attractions only eight members put in an appearance. The weather was all that could be desired and a very pleasant afternoon was spent at the pretty village.

Swimming.

On Tuesday night Warrington and Southport met in the Warrington baths in a Liverpool and District League second division match, an exciting game resulting in a victory for the home club by four goals to two. Warrington defended the shallow end in the first half, and C. Ashton opened the scoring after a minute's play. Allen next tested Bebbington, while W. Ashton hit the post. With his next shot, however, Ashton found the net. Warrington were penalised close in, and as a result T. R. Hillsdon scored, making the score at the interval Warrington two, Southport one. Changing ends, War- rington still held the advantage, and it was not long ere C. Ashton put the home side further ahead with a fine straight shot. Nevin immediately afterwards reduced the lead, but the local captain again beat Bebbington, and time arrived leaving Warrington winners by two clear goals.—Warrington beat Southport in a squadron race, after a most exciting tussle, by a " toucher." A two-lengths schoolboys' handicap proved very interest- ing. Results :—Heat 1 : 1, R. Beaman, go at 32 ;2, P. Chalmers, 29. Heat 2 : 1, A. Pemberton, 22 ;2, R. Atherton (311 and H. Cricket.

Taken all round, the season just concluded has been a successful one for Warrington. The League contests have tended to considerably increase the interest in the summer sport, and the form displayed by the eleven in these fixtures has been such as to please their supporters. Of course, it was a great disappointment not to secure the West Lancashire cup—and that by the narrowest of margins. The eleven played uncommonly well through- out the competition, bat that one defeat at Earlestown lost them the championship. To deal with the season's records, twenty matches altogether were played, and of these victories were gained in nine, four matcnes were lost, and the remaining seven were drawn. Warrington scored 2,116 runs for the loss of 161 wickets which works out at 15 runs per wicket, whilst the opposition have lost 167 wickets for 2,131 runs, the average being 12•76 per wicket. The success of the second team, who have won the championship of the Second Division of the West Lancashire League, will be hailed with pleasure,and their performances have been so consistently ' good that few will grudge them the honour. With regard to ordinary first eleven matches,Preston had the distinc- tion of dismissing the Warringtonians for the smallest aggregate of the season-15. Against this Warrington got rid of Newton for 43. The highest aggregate made was 211 for nine wickets against Wigan, who were also defeated at the colliery enclosure in the return engage- ment. Bowdon scored the most freely off the Warring- ton bowlers, notching 201 for the loss of five wickets.

Coming to individual performances, Cresswell, the professional, has done yeoman service, and he has the satisfaction of winning the gold medal of the West Lancashire League for the best bowling average. T. Wright has acquitted himself remarkably well with the bat, his aggregate for the season being 300. A word of praise is due to J. W. Bibby, the popular captain, for his judicious handling of the resources at his command. The sound fielding of the team has been an important factor towards the successes achieved. Duckworth has done some very smart work at the wicket. it was to be regretted that a serious accident at Bowdon on June 30th incapicaterl P. B. Ilawtin, a very reliable batsman, from furthur service. ---- Appended' are the batting averages and bowling analyses : FIRST ELEVEN'.

Bowling:

One stage in connection with the Works Bowling Association programme was concluded on Saturday, (when the team competitions were decided) and a large entry for the individual main promises to afford a fitting wind-up to a successful season. Rylands (No. 1) and Fletchers were the teams to indulge in the battle royal and their meeting attracted a large crowd to the bowling green attached to the Bowling Green Inn. One of the keenest struggles on record was anticipated and a wave of expectation went round as F. Nliller and E. Bailey opened the game. Rvlands man at the outset played Indifferently but pullin4 himself together ran from 0-2 to 9-3, and to 13-6. Bailey notched another couple when he had to retire beaten 15-3.

The lead which Rylands gained on the first game they never once relinquished, and their men were always masters of the situation. Fletcher's worked hard, but at no time could they reduce the margin to less than eleven points, and in the end their opponents carried off the Finlayson cup by 20 points. During the play some first class games were witnessed, and one of these lay between W. Daniels and J. Broadhurst. Fletcher's man opened with three singles and Daniels followinc, suit, the scores were brought level. Broadhurst again went to the front, but his opponent was not to be denied and peeled at 8,9, 11, 12 and 14, though he lost the last point. Boughley had an equally prolonged fight, though he met with greater 51CC1396 than his collertene. At the outset he was left very much in the rear by Woods, but getting his length he gradually drew level, and on the last end claimed the game. After leading 7-0 A. Shaw only defeated Bandies by the odd point. At one stage he even looked like being beaten as his opponent took the lead but Shaw is not the one to admit defeat before actually beaten and a determined effort gave him the honours. On the whole Rylands thoroughly deserved their victory and the first to admit this will be the defeated team though Fletchers mean, if the teams meet again nest year, to reverse the result.

The competition for the consolation prizes wee also decided on Saturday when Whitecross (No. 1) team carried everything before them. In the semi-final Rylands (.No 4) met the victors and had to bow the knee. Whitecross flatly declined to consider the opposi- tion over whom they rode to victory rough shod, scor- ing the highest possible number of points. In tlie final they had to meet Longford who had qualified at the , expense of Orford Tannery. In this meeting also they carried all before them and repeated their earlier per- formance. This N the way one likes to see matches won, where there is nothing half hearted but a deter- mination which surmounts all obstacles. The play of the Whit,ecross men was the feature of the afternoon sad their display was indeed entertaining.

A SOAP WAR. Increased Wholesale Prices and the Coupon System. WARRINGTON GROCERS' VIEWS

It is evident that the retail trade resent most strongly the deternaination of certain of the large wholesale soap-making firms in-the North of England to advance the prices to the dealers, as announced a fortnight ago. The decision has already called forth the strongest protests. At a meeting in Manchester of the Northern Council of Grocers' Associations a long and animated discussion took place on the subject. 1 One member suggested I that the Council should I ignore the manufacturers and introduce a soap of their own; which, he contended, if pushed by the members, would meet with a ready sale. On the other hand, it was pointed out that the power of advertising was so great nowadays that the grocers were practically compelled to handle certain brands of soap, and the suggestion to manufacture a " council " or "associa- tion " soap was regarded with clabimis feelings. The chief grievance appeared to be that the increased prices would only affect the retailers, and it was sub- mitted that the manufacturers should either modify or abolish their elaborate prize-giving system, or make arrangements whereby the advanced rates were equally borne by the public. A'resolution was adopted,suggest- ing a conference between the manufacturers and the leading officials of the Federation. Meantime, the controversy has been brought directly to the notice of the housewife. In the last day or two it has been noticed by them that grocers have refused to accept accumulated soap wrappers, in accordance with the popular scheme adopted by the manufacturers. The latter have met this with a counter move, and their representatives have been msking house-to-house visits, for the purpose of exchanging soap for wrappers. A representative of the" Examiner " made inquiries this (Friday) morning as to the views of Warrington grocers on the advance. He ascertained that at a recent meeting of the Warrington Grocers' and Provision Dealers' Association it was decided to send a letter to four of the largest firms—Messrs. Joseph Crostield and Sons Ltd., Lever Bros.. J. Watson and Son, and Gossages Limited—expressing the opinion that the retail price of llb tablets of their proprietary soaps should be advanced from 3d. to 3id. or the 3d tablets should be made of lesser weight, as the present price was not remunerative enough to members of the whole- sale or retail trade and was causing general dissatisfac- tion. The Warrington Association was one of the first to take action and letters have been received from the Manchester and Birkenhead Associations expressing agreement with the above opinion. A prominent Warrington grocer was emphatic in telling our reporter that the price to the consumer should be raised or the tablet made smaller. At the same time he was willing to concede that the advance was not a "game" of the soap-makers to go in for more profits. At present the supply of tallow— which largely comes from abroad—is not equal to the demand and consequently high prices rule. He believed ,that if the tallow market maintained its present condi- tion, the soap-makers would be bound to advance the retail prices. The profits which ought to be distributed between the consumer and the retailer were not, he considered, properly divided.
