REVISING THE LISTS. MR. COUGH'S UNSUCCESSFUL APPLICATION.

SEVEN YEARS DISFRANCHISEMENT.

Mr. James T. Foard, barrister-at-law, attended at the Town Hall, on Monday for the purpose of revising the lists of voters for the borough. The Conservatives were represented by Mr. Wm. Holbrook and Mr. Herbert Haves, and the Liberals by Mr. Joseph Howes, San. Mr. W. H. Woodcock, assistant overseer, was present, as well as Messrs. T. J. Garnett and F. H. Auk- land, relieving officers. Others present included the Mayor (Alderman Bolton), Alderman Smethurst, Coun- cillor Wilkinson, Mr. S. Wright, and Mr. T. S. Spinks, (assistant town clerk.) At the opening of the Court on Monday Mr. S.Gough applied for the removal of his narde from the list of persons convicted under the Corrupt Practices Act and for his re-instatement on the list of voters. Mr. Foard at the outset thanked Mr. Gough for haying supplied him beforehand with a list of the points he desired to raise ashe had been thereby enabled to look the matter up. He took it that Mr. Gough did not challenge that there were four charges of corruption against him under the Corrupt Practices Act and that he was duly convicted. Mr. Gough intimated that he did not. ' The Barrister : It is rather a novel case, and one of undoubted importance. I understand that what you wish to raise is whether the fact of conviction by itself involves disfranchisement?

Mr. Gough : Yes, sir. The Barrister : There is one point 7011 have raised. I may draw attention to the Act of 1883, which provides that a person who is convicted under section 6," on indictment of any corrupt practice shall, in addition to any punishment be not capable during a period of seven years from the period of his conviction of being registered or of holding a public office," and in addition he cannot be registered as an 'elector or voter at any election in the United Kingdom, whether it he at a Parliamentary election, or an election for any public office within the meaning of that Act. The Act of 1881 is the one under which we have to act really, and it says : "A person guilty of an illegal practice in reference to a municipal election shall, on summary conviction, be liable to a fine not exceeding £lOO, and be incapable during a period of five years from the date of his conviction of being regis- tered as an elector or voting at any election, whether it be a Parliamentary election or an election for a public office within the meaning of this Act; held for or within the borough in which the illegal practice has been committed." It is upon that particu- lar section that the Town Clerk has, in pursuance of his duty, under a penalty of £5O, to make the return which the statute provides, and this return is before me, and which I am, by this Act, entitled to adjudicate upon, and it is to the effect that Samuel Gough, house- furnisher,Winwick-street,was found guilty of the offence of bribery (at a Borough Council election) at the Liver- pool Assizes, on the 16th February, 1901. If the Town Clerk had not inserted the name it would have been my duty to have called upon you by special notice to attend here to say why your name should not be put in the illegal practices list. I merely draw your attention to the disqualification. Ido not know that I can do any more than draw your attention to the Act of 1884 which declares positively and absolutely that a person guilty of an illegal practice in reference to a municipal elec- tion shall on summary conviction be liable to a fine not exceeding £lOO. In your case I suppose the judge con- sidered there were such mitigating circumstances to be considered that he only fined you £25. Mr. Gough : He said they had not taken a petition. The Barrister: That is merely an alternative pro- vision : they chose that particular mode. The judge himself trying the case would have no power to deal with this particular point of your disfranchisement. That follows ipso facto on the conviction. linfortun- lately I can do nothing in the circumstances. I cannot Igo behind the statute, which is emphatic. I cannot inquire whether the decision is right or not. I have to 'act on that decision. Mr. Gough : Don't you think it necessary they should produce the certificate of conviction? The Barrister: I have no power at all. Mr. Gough : It is not stated in the section that a certificata of the Court shall be presented ? The Barrister : That merely deals with cases under election petitions. All those sections, or nearly all, have been repealed. Mr. Cough: My contention is that the only way to proceed against one charged with bribery or corruption is by election petition. It is on the finding of an election court. I know no case. I have solicitors who have looked the cases up,and they tell me they know no case similar to mine where there has been only one pro- ceeding under summary jurisdiction. The Barrister : So far there has not been a case, precisely the same. lam not aware of a particular case in which there has been a conviction before a judge,or properly constituted authority for the particular offence, but where that conviction has taken place, and there is a copy of the conviction placed before me as there is in this case, I have— Mr. Goagh : That is what lam challenging. The Barrister: I have a certified copy under the statute from Sir Herbert Stephen,clerk to the Liverpool Assizes, dated Eeptember 7th,1906. The Barrister then read the text of the certificate, which concluded by stating that " the conviction still remained in full force, and had not been reversed or made void." That was absolute and final. Mr. Gough : I made an application to the Town Clerk, but this was not the certificate that was pro- duced. I should like to know when the certificate was obtained. Mr. T. S. Spinks (assistant Town 'Clerk).—There can be no objection to Mr. Gough knowing when we received the certificate. We obtained it last week. We have the previous one in our possession—the one we got at Mr. Cough's conviction. The Barrister : That will do Mr. Gough. Mr. Gough : Are you prepared to state a case ? The Barrister: I don't think there is any rule. __ . .

Mr. Gough: I think there is. The Barrister : There is no rule upon which I could state a case, or I would do so. I must leave you to your ordinary remedies. If your advisers think there is a case they must take the usual course to enforce me stating it. That is all. Mr. Gough : It is for five years. The Barrister : For five years and not seven. I don't know whether an appeal could have been made to the judge. I apprehend not from the words of the Act. Mr. Gough (shaking hands with Mr. Foard) : I am very much obliged to you for the kindness yomhave shown to me, and for the efforts you have made to try and explain the matter. SEVEN YEARS' DISFRANCHISEMENT, NOT FIVE. At the evening sitting, the Revising Barrister again asked for Mr. Gough, but that gentleman was not Present. Mr. Foard said he was under a misapprehen- sion that morning, when he was dealing with the case, believing that Mr.Gough was convicted under a particular section of the Act, which said that five years was the maximum punishment. He found, however, on reference to the statute that Mr. Gough was convicted under section 6, and the consequence was that he was dis- qualified for seven years, and not five. MR. PETER PEACOCK'S VOTE.

Mr. Peter Peacock applied for a vote as tenant of 62 West-street. Mr. Holbrook : Does anyone reside with you at West-street ? Applicant : My brother and my sister. It is my brother's house and I am the tenant. Mr. Holbrook objected, and said so many resided in the house that it was hard to find out who was the tenant. Mr. A.PeaPock was on the lists in respect to the same house. Mr. Howes : Mr. Holbrook objects to Mr. Arthur Peacock having the vote on the ground that he is not the tenant. The applicant is the tenaat. Questioned by the Barrister and others, Mr. Peacock said he lived in Bewsev-road until the end of September when he went to 62, West-street. His brother was the owner of the house. Mr. Holbrook said that the applicant did not reside at 62, West-street, but in the Wallasey division. The Barrister : I shall want some proof that the applicant pays the rent, and that this is not a family arranaement. Applicant : I can produce the receipts for the rent. Continuing, he said he had taken apartments at Wallasey for the summer months, and he went there three or four nights a week according as his business allowed, but he was bound to stay at West-street for so many days. Mr. Howes asked for the case to be adjourned until the Evening when the rent receipts could be produced. He added that the applicant went to Wallasey for the benefit of his health. He took a summer residence near the seaside. As a matter of fact for four or five nir hts a week he regularly slept at 62, West-street, of which he was the tenant. He paid the rent to his brother who was the landlord of the house. The receipts would be produced in the evening. Mr. Holbrook: Will they be written to-day? Mr. Howes : I refuse to discuss that with you. Mr. Peacock has paid the rent and receipts have been given and those receipts will be produced. The claim was allowed. APPLICATION BY MR. ENGLAND.

Mr. Ebenezer England, accountant, &c., and one of the borough auditors, applied for a vote in respect of an office in Corporation-street, which, he said, be had occupied sinct the let March, 1901. He paid £lO a year rent. Mr. Holbrook objected on the ground that Mr. England had given up the tenancy during the qualifying period. Mr. England said he gave notice to give up the tenancy on March Ist of this year, but he subsequently renewed the tenancy. He never took the furniture out of the office, and, therefore, he claimed thathe had been paving rent continuously as from the Ist March. Mr. Holbrook : He really gave up the premises. He gave notice to the overseers that he was not to be rated during a portion of the year.

STOCK AND SHARE MARKETS.

Mr. England : Excuse me. I never gave notice to the overseers that I was not to be rated. The Barrister: You have paid for the whole of the twelve months?

Mr. England : Yes, sir, as I can prove. The Barrister : He has not paid the rate in respect of the quarter for which he gave notice. Mr. Howee : Because the rates are not yet due for the quarter. The quarter is not yet up. The Assistant Overseer : It is a half-yearly rate. Mr. England has up to the end of September in which to pay. The Barrister : It is contemplated to make him pay the rates. lam satisfied about the tenancy, and I want to_be satisfied about the rates. _

Mr. Holbrook (to the Assistant Overseer) : Did you get any notice that he was to be rated ? Mr. Woodward: We presumed that the office had been vacated. The vote was allowed. VOTE CLAIMED FOR MR. ARTHUR PFIACOCR".. "ATTEMPT TO MANUFACTURE A VOTE."

Mr. Peter Peacock applied on behalf of hie brother Mr. Arthur Peacock, for a vote in respect of a ware- house in \Vest-street. Mr. Holbrook said that that was the third time Mr. Peter Peacock had applied for votes that day in connection with the family, first for himself, then for a brother, and now for another brother: _

Mr. peacock: But not all for the same place. Each case is entirely separate your Honour. Mr. Holbrook contended that Mr. Arthur Peacock had not been the tenant for the qualifying time, and that he purchased the business from the trustees of the late firm of A. F. and S. A. Peacock, of West-street. He could not for reasons which Mr. Holbrook stated have been in occupation on the 15th July as alleged by Mr. Peter Peacock.

The Barrister said he must have better evidence. ,Mr. Holbrook : Perhaps the Assistant Overseer can give us the date of the occupation. The reply was that it was the Ist of October. The Barrister : There may have been some sort of understanding amongst the firm, but I can only accept the overseer's testimony as being final. Mr. Howes : If Mr. Arthur Peacock appears will you re-consider the matter ? The Barrister : I don't mind hearing him, but I don't think I shall be able to alter the facts. _ _ . .

Mr. Holbrook : This is nothing more than a dis- gricefill attempt to manufacture a vote. The Barrister : It does not appear to commend itself to me at present.

The claim waS disallowed. CONCLT_TDING SITTING.

The concluding sitting of the Court was held on Wednesday morning, when the process known as starring" the lists was gone through. The Conservatives claimed a lodger's vote on behalf of Mr. Edward Draper, solicitor. Mr. Howes pointed out that there were two occu- pants, and the rental was £l5 10s. net. The Barrister held the claim to be a good one and allowed it.

At the close of the business Mr. W. Holbrook, on behalf of the Conservative Association, moved a rote of thanks to the Revising Barrister for the kindly manner in which he had listened to all'cases,and for the judgments he had given. He also cordially thanked the overseers who had succeeded in producing a very good list.

Mr. 3. Howes, jun., on behalf of the Liberal Associa- tion and Progressive Labour League, seconded. Since the last Revision Court, he said, they had been through the heat of an election and it was surprising that during the contest not a single complaint was made at the Liberal offices by any person who thought he was entitled to be on the list but had been left off. That fact, he thought, spoke volumes for the overseers. It was a pleasure to be associated with Mr. Woodcock and he did not think they could find a more perfect register in any part of the country than the one they had in Warrington. The kindness extended to those who had business at the Court by the Revising Barrister made it a pleasure to appear before him. Mr. Foard in responding said he had received the greatest possible assistance from both political agents and he could only echo the words of Mr. Howes with respect to the excellence of the lists. They were as nearly perfect as he could conceive them to be made. He had to congratulate himself on having been saved an enormous amount of trouble and the ratepayers had also been saved trouble in not having to appear in answer to objections on errors. He wished to specially acknow- ledge the assistance of Mr. Woodcock in preparing such admirable lists.

Mr. Woodcock said he was much obliged for the complimentary remarks about the lists. It had been his endeavour and the endeavour of the staff to prepare as perfect lits as possible and they left no stone unturned to achieve that object. It was gratifying to find that the result of their labours was so satisfactory to the Revising Barrister and to the public.

FLOWER SHOW AT WINWICK.

Success attended the second annual show in connec- tion with the Winwick Horticultural Society which was held on Saturday in the Schools, Winwick. The number and quality of the exhibits showed a decided improvement, and great interest was taken in the show. Notable features were the fine dis- plays of cut flowers. fruit and vegetables. The President of the society is the Rev. Canon Penrhvn ; the Vice-President, the Rev. A. Inglis ; the Hon. Secretary. Mr. J. S. Pettener ; and the committee Messrs. J. Holmes, R. Jolley, G. W. Beard, F. Britch, C. Geron, Preston. P. Longton, J. Platt, A. Baker, Berry, R. Melling, and Greenhalgh.

THE AWARDS.

The following is a list of the awards : Plants.—Foliage plants, distinct : 1, G. W. Beard ; 2, J. E. Pettener. Window plants (flowering) distinct : 1, G. W. Beard; 2, .T. E. Pettener. WESLEYAN QUARTERLY MEETING. Proposed Supernumerary. Minister for

, * - 4 • n „.

Lymm. The quarterly meeting in connection with the War- rington Wesleyan Circuit was held at Lytnin on Thurs- day afternoon. Tea was served in the schoolroom, the attendance numbering bet Ween forty and fifty, and after- wards the business of the Circuit was discussed. The financial report presented by Mr. J. G. Withinshaw was very satisfactory. After paying the expenses there was a surplus on the quarter's work of Rll, and it was decided to devote this to the circuit debt.

- The membership returns were submitted. and showed the number of members in the circuit to be 899—an increase of 2—with 92 on trial, Mr. William Silcock was appointed treasurer of the Foreign Missions Fund, and Mr. George Weaver, junr., was elected lay secretary. Arrangements were made for a Foreign Missionary Campaign in the winter. Mr. William Hickman was appointed treasurer of the Circuit Travelling Fund. The Institution Committee reported that the Rev. Thomas Gladding of Bradford had accepted the invita- tion of the circuit to come to Warrington in 1908 as second minister.

A long discussion took place es to the desirability of engaging a supernumerary minister to reside at Lymm The suggestion was very favourably received by the meeting and a committee was appointed to arrange the financial details. , Mr. J. E. Richardson was elected treasurer of the Worn-out Preschers' Fund.

_ It"-Was—d-eCided to hold the next quarterly meeting at Bold-street Church on December 13th.

W ARRINGTON 'UNION. APPOINTMENT OF VALUBR. The Guardians of the Warrington Union IFIVITH APPLI- CATIONS FOR THE OFFICE OF VALURR OF RATE- ABLE HEREDITAMENTS inclusive of Railways, Canals, manufactories &c., within the Union. The person to be appointed must have had experience in the work and each Candidate must explain in his application the nature and extent thereof. He will be at liberty to accept retainers for valuation work within other Poor Law Ut,lons but will not be permitted to undertake work of any kind within the Warrington Union other than that of the Assessment Com- mittee. Remuneration £2OO per annum. Applications with testimonials to be received by .me on or before Saturday September 29th at 12 noon. By Order, J. C. SUTTPIT, Clerk to the Assessment Committee. Bewley Chambers, Warrington, September, 1906.

If in doubt, send 2s. 9d. for one box, A Warrington Man in Trouble. or 13s. 9d. for six, to the British Depot, 46, Holbome- A young man, who gave his name as William Kelly, viaduct, London.. of Warrington, was charged at the Ormskirk Police Court, on Monday with being drunk and riotous, and further with assaulting Nicholas Dunk, licensee of the Dray and Horses Inn, Ormskirk. The evidence showed that on Saturday afternoon prisoner, who was drunk, went into the public-house and was refused drink. He declined to leave, and on the landlord ejecting him he struck him on the forehead. He afterwards left, and Mr. Dunk complained to the police, and assisted Police-Constable Scragg to convey him to the police station from another part of the town. Kelly was fined 7s. 6d. and costs, or 14 days, for being drunk and riotous' and was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour for the assault, the sentences to run con- secutively, • Foresters' Picnic to Belle Vue. The annual trip to Belle Vne in connection with the Warrington district of the Ancient Order of Foresters took place on Saturday when nearly 200 members enjoyed themselves in the gardens, in the best of weather. The members had tea provided in a separate room, and were joined there by H.C.T, Bro. Hulse, Chester, also D.C.R. and D.S. Bros. Daulby and Farrow, of Manchester. All the chief officials from Warrington were present, including D.C.R. Bro. Peacock, D.S. Bro. Davies, P.D.C.R's. Bros. G. Maybury, Hipkiss, Chrimes, Hill and Hankinson, also trustees Bros. Smith, Walsh and James. After tea a few remarks were made by the visitors Bros. Hulse Farrow and Daulby, and they were thanked by Bro. Peacock. The fireworks display was much appreciated, and afterwards the members returned by special train arriving at Warrington at 10-40 p.m. A New Warrington Company. We learn that Mersey-street tannery (for many years carried on by Messrs. I. and J. Tinsley) but which has been shut down for about twelve months, is shortly to be in full swing once more. The concern has been acquired by a new company, registered this week as the Warrington Tanning Co., Ltd.' with a capital ofchairmanl3o,ooo. Mr. James Dodds is chairman of directors, Mr. Aaron Appleton (Litherland) managing director, and Mr. John Hankinson, secretary. ,The statutory seven subscribers to the company are : —Mr. James Dodds, Mr, Wm. Walker (Whitehaven), Mr. R. H. Posnett (Runcorn), Mr. Aaron Appleton, Mrs. Appleton, Mr. John Hankinson, and Mr, Charles E. Parker. Operations will be commenced immediately, and the tannery, which is one of the largest in the district, should cause an improvement in the local labour market. We hope the new venture will turn out a success, and thus strengthen Warrington's already powerful position in the leather trade. 3, R. WOOD, Piano Dealer and Tuner, 47, Old London-road., Stockton Heath.—Advt. Through Mid-Cheshire by Motor. The beautiful scenery and fine roads of Cheshire are famed everywhere, and it is not surprising that motoring in the county is so popular. Ott Monday afternoon a party of Warrington journalists enjoyed a trip through some of the most charming spots in Cheshire, a handsomely-appointed motor waggonette being placed at their disposal by the Warrington Motor Carriage Co., Ltd. Leaving the town via Wilderspool the Tarporley main road through Dela- mere Forest, with its rich foliage and heather and bracken, was first taken. The Manchester to Chester main road was crossed, and a few miles further on, the party turned down the pretty highway to Winsford. The pleasant village of Over, with its picturesquely-situated church, was the next place of interest and afterwards Winsford and Middlewich— typical Cheshire salt towns—were passed in quick succession. The ancient town of Sandbath was reached in good time and here tea was served. The visitors viewed the famous crosses and also ascended the tower of the old Parish Church from which a splendid view was obtained. Continuing the journey, Crewe was visited and then the route was taken for Holmes Chapel and Knutsford. After an exhilarating spin along the Knutsford-road, the party arrived at Warrington about ten o'clock, the outing being voted a complete success. The motor waggon- ette, which seemed to be regarded as a novelty throughout the district which it traversed, ran most satisfactorily. •Linws MOWERS, Garden Tools, &c. Splendid selection.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-st.—Advt. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. In their report to be presented at the thirty-third annual meeting of the shareholders of this company on September 27th, the directors state :—The profits for the year ending June 30th, 1906, amount to £62,866 15s, 8d.., and the balance brought forward from last year is £B,OBl lie. 4d., together 5:10,948 7s. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent, per annum on the preference shares and 4 per cent. on the ordinary shares was paid in March last fcr the half-year ending December 31st, 1905, amounting to £19,325. The directors now recommend that for the past half-year a dividend at the rate per annum of 6 per cent, on the preference shares and 6 per cent. on the ordinary shares be declared, making a total dis- tribution for the year of 144,760. The directors further recommend that £lO,OOO be added to the reserve fund, that the sum of £6,874 19s. 4d., which represents the expenditure on capital account for the year, be written off, and that the balance of £9,323 7s. Bd. be carried forward. During the past year the output of the collieries has been well maintained, but prices have continued to rule on a very low basis. In the iron and stenl trades the works have been fully employed, and a higher range of prices has been established. A rise in wages and raw material has, however, fully counterbalanced this advantage. The engineering trade has been busy in all departments, and the orders and contracts on hand hold out a prospect of full work for some timef to come. Important additions and developments have taken place in this branch of the Company's business. Mr. Beck, the senior member of the Board, has decided to retire, on account of advancing years, and the directors in communicating his decision to the share- holders wish to put on record their high appreciation of the valuable services he has rendered to the Com- pany ever since its formation, as well as the great personal esteem in which he is held by them all. In compliance with the Articles of Association,Mr.W.Peter Rylands retires from the Board, and is eligible for re-election. The auditors Messrs. Blease and Sons, also retire, and are eligible for re-election.

Supposed Ineendiarism at Thelwall. At about eight o'clock on Monday morning, the Warrington Fire Brigade was summoned to a fire which had broken out on the farm premises of Mr. E. W. Reynolds, Massey Hall, Thelwall. A contingent of firemen, in charge of Deputy Superintendent Turner, with the manual engine and other applicances at once proceeded there, and found a stack of hay on fire. A plentiful supply of water was obtained from the mains in the roadway, and the men got to work and pre- vented the fire spreading to the adjoining stacks, and afterwards were engaged in cutting away and turning over the smouldering debris. The damage is estimated at £7O and is covered by insurance. The fire is supposed to be the work of an incendiary.
